A thermodynamically consistent model for bulk-surface viscous fluid mixtures: Model derivation and mathematical analysis
Patrik Knopf, Jonas Stange

Abstract




This is a preprint version of the paper. Please cite as:

P. Knopf, J. Stange,

[Journal]

xx
:xx 000-000 (2024),

https://doi.org/...

Abstract

We derive and analyze a new diffuse interface model for incompressible, viscous fluid mixtures with bulk-surface interaction.
Our system consists of a Navier–Stokes–Cahn–Hilliard model in the bulk that is coupled to a surface Navier–Stokes–Cahn–Hilliard model on the boundary.
Compared with previous models, the inclusion of an additional surface Navier–Stokes equation is motivated, for example, by biological applications such as the seminal
fluid mosaic model
(Singer & Nicolson,
Science
, 1972) in which the surface of biological cells is interpreted as a thin layer of viscous fluids.
We derive our new model by means of local mass balance laws, local energy dissipation laws, and the Lagrange multiplier approach.
Moreover, we prove the existence of global weak solutions via a semi-Galerkin discretization.
The core part of the mathematical analysis is the study of a novel bulk-surface Stokes system and its corresponding bulk-surface Stokes operator. Its eigenfunctions are used as the Galerkin basis to discretize the bulk-surface Navier–Stokes subsystem.

Keywords:
Multi-phase flows, Navier–Stokes–Cahn–Hilliard system, bulk-surface interaction, dynamic boundary conditions, bulk-surface Stokes system, bulk-surface Stokes operator.

Mathematics Subject Classification:

Primary: 76T06 ; Secondary:
35D30, 35Q35, 76D03, 76D05, 76D07, 76T99.

1  Introduction

The mathematical description of two immiscible materials is an important topic in materials science and fluid dynamics with vast applications in biology, chemistry, and engineering. To describe the motion of such mixtures, one has to capture the moving interface separating the different components. This has led to two fundamental approaches, namely the
sharp interface methods
and the
diffuse interface methods
. For a comparison of these two approaches, we refer, e.g., to
[
4
,
23
,
35
,
64
]
.

In sharp interface models, the interface is described as an evolving hypersurface in the surrounding domain, which leads to the formulation of a free boundary problem. In contrast, in diffuse interface models, the interface between the two materials is represented by a thin layer whose width is proportional to a small parameter
\varepsilon>0
. The location of the two components is represented by an
order parameter
, also called
phase-field
. This phase-field describes the difference of the local concentrations of the materials, and is therefore expected to attain values close to
-1
and
1
in the regions where the respective fluids are present. On the other hand, at the diffuse interface, it should have a continuous transition between these values. The advantage of the diffuse interface method is that the description of the phase-field is purely Eulerian and does not require any direct tracking of the interface separating the involved materials. Moreover, it allows for topology changes of the material components and has a firm physical basis for multiphase flows, see, e.g.,
[
57
,
18
]
.

1.1  Diffuse interface models for multi-phase flows

To describe the motion of two viscous, incompressible fluids with constant densities, one of the most fundamental models is the
Model H
, which was originally proposed by Hohenberg and Halperin in
[
45
]
in the context of dynamic critical phenomena. It consists of an incompressible Navier–Stokes equation coupled to a convective Cahn–Hilliard equation and was later rigorously derived in
[
42
]
. The fundamental assumption in its derivation is that the density of the mixture is constant, which means that both fluids have the same constant density.
In the past two decades, various generalizations of the Model H have been proposed in the literature to describe fluids with different densities, compressible fluids, or phase-transitions. We refer to
[
8
,
9
,
15
,
22
,
28
,
35
,
43
,
58
,
70
,
71
,
77
]
for a selection of representative contributions.

A significant advance was achieved by the work of Abels, Garcke, and Grün
[
1
]
, who derived a thermodynamically consistent diffuse-interface model for two-phase flows with different densities. The so-called
AGG model
generalizes the Model H by incorporating non-matched densities, and it ensures energy dissipation, frame-indifference, and a consistent sharp-interface limit. It has since become the basis for many different works on diffuse-interface models of incompressible two-phase flows, see, e.g.,
[
2
,
3
,
5
,
36
,
37
]
.

In this framework, the treatment of boundary conditions plays a crucial role. Classical choices are no-slip boundary conditions for the velocity field, while the Cahn–Hilliard variables are usually equipped with homogeneous Neumann boundary conditions. However, numerous applications demonstrate that these static boundary conditions are insufficient when an accurate description of interfacial behaviour near the boundary is required. For instance, in the modelling of moving contact lines, the use of the generalized Navier boundary condition, relating tangential stresses to slip velocities and uncompensated Young stresses, has proven indispensable, see, e.g.,
[
24
,
66
,
69
]
. Similarly, imposing homogeneous Neumann boundary conditions on the phase-field forces the diffuse interface to intersect the boundary at a right angle. This is often unrealistic, as in most applications, the contact angle deviates from ninety degrees and may even evolve dynamically over time. Furthermore, the homogeneous Neumann boundary condition prohibits any transfer of material between the bulk and the boundary. While such conservation of bulk mass is appropriate in some situations, other applications, such as absorption phenomena or chemical reaction at the boundary, require an exchange of material between bulk and surface. These limitations have also been recognized in the physics literature (see, e.g.,
[
14
,
26
,
27
,
48
]
), where the introduction of a surface free energy has been proposed to better capture short-range interactions between the bulk and the boundary.

To overcome these limitations, Navier–Stokes–Cahn–Hilliard models with dynamic boundary conditions on the Cahn–Hilliard subsystem and a generalized Navier-slip boundary condition on the velocity field have been proposed in the literature.
To motivate our new bulk-surface Navier–Stokes–Cahn–Hilliard model, which will be derived and analyzed in the present paper, we first recall the model from
[
38
]
, which reads as follows:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\rho(\phi)\mathbf{v})+\textnormal{div}(\mathbf{v}\otimes(\rho(\phi)\mathbf{v}+\mathbf{J}))=\textnormal{div}\;\mathbf{T},

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{div}\;\mathbf{v}=0

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q,

(1.1a)

\displaystyle[\mathbf{T}\mathbf{n}+\gamma(\psi)\mathbf{v}]_{\tau}=\big[-\psi\nabla_{\Gamma}\theta+\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathbf{J}\cdot\mathbf{n})\mathbf{v}\big]_{\tau}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma,

(1.1b)

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{n}=0

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma,

(1.1c)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\phi+\textnormal{div}(\phi\mathbf{v})=\textnormal{div}(m_{\Omega}(\phi)\nabla\mu)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q,

(1.1d)

\displaystyle\mu=-\varepsilon\Delta\phi+\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}F^{\prime}(\phi)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q,

(1.1e)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\psi+\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(\psi\mathbf{v})=\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(m_{\Gamma}(\psi)\nabla_{\Gamma}\theta)-\beta m_{\Omega}(\phi)\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\mu

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma,

(1.1f)

\displaystyle\theta=-\delta\kappa\Delta_{\Gamma}\psi+\tfrac{1}{\delta}G^{\prime}(\psi)+\varepsilon\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\phi

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma,

(1.1g)

\displaystyle\phi=\psi,\hskip 20.00003ptLm_{\Omega}(\phi)\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\mu=\beta\theta-\mu,

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002ptL\in[0,\infty]

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

(1.1h)

We also refer to
[
21
]
for a related Cahn–Hilliard–Brinkman model with dynamic boundary conditions, and to
[
52
]
for a free boundary variant of (
1.1
), where the fluids are considered in an evolving domain, whose evolution is driven by the velocity field. A model similar to (
1.1
) was previously investigated in
[
30
,
31
]
. It involves a second-order Allen–Cahn type dynamic boundary condition instead of (
1.1f
)-(
1.1g
) to account for the contact angle dynamics.

In system (
1.1
),
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
d\in\{2,3\}
is a bounded domain with boundary
\Gamma\coloneqq\partial\Omega
,
T>0
is a prescribed final time, and we set
Q=\Omega\times(0,T)
and
\Sigma=\Gamma\times(0,T)
for brevity. The outward unit normal vector field on
\Gamma
is denoted by
\mathbf{n}
, while
\nabla_{\Gamma},\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}
and
\Delta_{\Gamma}
stand for the surface gradient, the surface divergence and the Laplace–Beltrami operator on
\Gamma
, respectively.
Furthermore,
\mathbf{v}:Q\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
denotes the bulk velocity field, which describes the motion of the mixture in
\Omega
. The location of the two materials in the bulk is represented by the bulk phase-field
\phi:Q\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. Its trace on the boundary
\Gamma
, the surface phase-field, is denoted by
\psi=\phi|_{\Gamma}:\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. The functions
\mu:Q\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
\theta:\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
stand for the bulk chemical potential and the surface chemical potential, respectively.

The time evolution of
\mathbf{v}
is described by the bulk Navier–Stokes equation (
1.1a
). The flow in the bulk is assumed to be incompressible, that is, the velocity field is divergence-free. The bulk Navier–Stokes equation is complemented with the boundary condition (
1.1b
), which can be regarded as an inhomogeneous Navier-slip boundary condition. It enhances the Navier-slip boundary condition previously proposed in
[
66
]
.
Here,
\gamma(\phi)
represents the slip parameter, which accounts for tangential friction at the boundary and may depend on the phase-field.
In (
1.1a
),
\rho(\phi)
stands for the phase-field dependent density in the bulk, and is given by

\displaystyle\rho(\phi)\coloneqq\frac{\tilde{\rho}_{2}-\tilde{\rho}_{1}}{2}\phi+\frac{\tilde{\rho}_{2}+\tilde{\rho}_{1}}{2}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q,

(1.2)

where
\tilde{\rho}_{1}
and
\tilde{\rho}_{2}
denote the individual constant densities of the two fluids, respectively. As the derivative of
\rho
is constant, we often write

\displaystyle\rho^{\prime}\equiv\rho^{\prime}(\phi)=\frac{\tilde{\rho}_{2}-\tilde{\rho}_{1}}{2}

for simplicity. Furthermore,
\mathbf{J}
represents the
mass flux
related to interfacial motion, and
\mathbf{T}
is the
stress tensor
. They are given by the formulas

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}

\displaystyle=-\rho^{\prime}\,m_{\Omega}(\phi)\nabla\mu

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q,

(1.3)

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{S}-p\mathbf{I}-\varepsilon\nabla\phi\otimes\nabla\phi\hskip 10.00002pt\text{with}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathbf{S}\coloneqq 2\nu_{\Omega}(\phi)\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q.

(1.4)

The coefficient
\nu_{\Omega}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is the viscosity of the mixtures in the bulk, which usually depends on the phase-field.
Provided that both fluids have a constant viscosity that is denoted by
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
, respectively, a typical choice is the affine interpolation

\displaystyle\nu_{\Omega}(\phi)=\frac{\nu_{2}-\nu_{1}}{2}\phi+\frac{\nu_{2}+\nu_{1}}{2}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q.

The matrix
\mathbf{S}
is referred to as the
viscous stress tensor
. It depends on the
symmetric gradient
of
\mathbf{v}
, which is defined as

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\big((\nabla\mathbf{v})+(\nabla\mathbf{v})^{\top}\big).

The time evolution of
\phi,\psi,\mu
and
\theta
is given by the
convective bulk-surface Cahn–Hilliard subsystem
(
1.1d
)-(
1.1h
), which consists of a
bulk Cahn–Hilliard equation
(
1.1d
)-(
1.1e
), that is coupled to a
surface Cahn–Hilliard equation
(
1.1f
)-(
1.1g
) through the normal derivatives
\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\phi
and
\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\mu
, as well as through the boundary conditions (
1.1h
), which has to be understood in the following sense:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\mu=\beta\theta&\text{if\penalty\ }L=0,\\
Lm_{\Omega}(\phi)\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\mu=\beta\theta-\mu&\text{if\penalty\ }L\in(0,\infty),\\
m_{\Omega}(\phi)\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\mu=0&\text{if\penalty\ }L=\infty,\end{cases}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

(1.5)

The functions
m_{\Omega},m_{\Gamma}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
are the so-called
Onsager mobilities
. In general, they may depend on the phase-field variables
\phi
and
\psi
, respectively, and describe both the spatial distribution and the magnitude of the underlying diffusion mechanisms.

The functions
F^{\prime}
and
G^{\prime}
are the derivatives of double-well potentials
F
and
G
, respectively. A physically motivated example of such a double-well potential, especially in applications related to materials science, is the
logarithmic potential
, which is also referred to as the
Flory–Huggins potential
. It is defined as

\displaystyle W_{\mathrm{log}}(s)=\frac{\Theta}{2}\big[(1+s)\ln(1+s)+(1-s)\ln(1-s)\big]-\frac{\Theta_{c}}{2}s^{2}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for\penalty\ }s\in[-1,1].

(1.6)

Here, we assume that
0<\Theta<\Theta_{c}
, where
\Theta
denotes the temperature of the mixture, and
\Theta_{c}
represents the critical temperature below which phase separation processes are to be expected. We point out that, since
W_{\mathrm{log}}^{\prime}(s)\rightarrow\pm\infty
as
s\rightarrow\pm 1
, the potential
W_{\mathrm{log}}
is classified as a singular potential.

Furthermore,
\varepsilon
is a parameter related to the thickness of the diffuse interface in the bulk, whereas
\delta>0
corresponds to the width of the diffuse interface on the boundary. The parameter
\kappa\geq 0
serves as a weight for the surface Dirichlet energy
\psi\mapsto\int_{\Gamma}\left|\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\right|^{2}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma
. If
\kappa>0
, this term has a regularizing effect on the phase separation at the boundary.

Although the model (
1.1
) allows for unmatched densities, variable contact angles, an improved description of contact line movement via the generalized Navier boundary condition (
1.1b
), and a transfer of material between bulk and surface (if
L<\infty
), it still exhibits some important limitations.

1.2  Limitations concerning the description of surface materials

In many applications from materials science, chemistry, engineering, or life sciences, the materials on the boundary might actually be different from those in the bulk. In such situations, the rheological properties of the surface materials often need to be taken into account, cf.
[
68
]
.

An important example is the classical
fluid-mosaic model
for the structure of cell membranes from the seminal work
[
74
]
. There, biological membranes are conceptualized as laterally incompressible, two-dimensional viscous fluid layers with embedded proteins.
This justifies the treatment of the boundary as an active viscous layer supporting both lateral momentum transport and compositional evolution.
These concepts were formalized mathematically in
[
11
]
as a continuum model, where lipid bilayers are described by a
surface Navier–Stokes equation
with positive, but finite viscosity, which is expected to dominate relaxation dynamics. These findings and observations motivate the inclusion of a
surface Navier–Stokes equation
in system (
1.1
) to account for viscous and convective effects at the boundary.

Figure 1:
Sketch of a domain (representing, e.g., a biological cell) with two phases (fluids) in the bulk and two phases (fluids) on the boundary, each separated by a diffuse interface.

However, if the materials on the boundary actually differ from those in the bulk,
there is no particular reason why a trace relation between
\phi
and
\psi
, as imposed by
\eqref{eqs:GK:8}_{1}
, should be fulfilled.
To account for the interaction of different materials, the more general transmission condition

\displaystyle\varepsilon K\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\phi=H(\psi)-\phi\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma

(1.7)

was proposed in
[
19
,
49
]
. It involves a parameter
K\in[0,\infty]
and a transmission function
H
.
Similar to the boundary condition on the chemical potentials, (
1.7
) is to be understood as

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\phi=H(\psi)&\text{if\penalty\ }K=0,\\
\varepsilon K\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\phi=H(\psi)-\phi&\text{if\penalty\ }K\in(0,\infty),\\
\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\phi=0&\text{if\penalty\ }K=\infty,\end{cases}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

(1.8)

The most popular choice is a linear transmission condition, which means that
H
is given by
H(s)=\alpha s
for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
and some constant
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
.

A further mathematical limitation of the model (
1.1
) is that, at least up to now, the existence of a global weak solution in the situation of unmatched densities is ensured only if
L\in(0,\infty]
(see
[
32
]
). In the case
L=0
, the existence of a global weak solution was established in
[
38
,
32
]
only in the case of matched densities.
Moreover, in all cases
L\in[0,\infty]
, the regularity of weak solutions to system (
1.1
) is rather limited. Regularity results for the bulk-surface convective Cahn–Hilliard subsystem (with prescribed velocity fields) have been obtained in
[
53
,
54
,
39
]
. However, these results are not applicable for weak solutions of (
1.1
) as the trace of the velocity field on the boundary is not regular enough. In the present paper, we will see that the additional inclusion of a surface Navier–Stokes equation helps to overcome this issue, as the inclusion of internal friction has a regularizing effect on the velocity field on the boundary. As discussed in Remark
4.3
, the regularity results established in
[
39
]
are actually applicable for weak solutions of our new model, and in this way, the regularity of the phase-fields
\phi
and
\psi
can be increased significantly.

1.3  A new bulk-surface Navier–Stokes–Cahn–Hilliard model

In the present paper, we derive and analyze a new bulk-surface Navier–Stokes–Cahn–Hilliard model, which reads as follows:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\rho(\phi)\mathbf{v})+\textnormal{div}(\mathbf{v}\otimes(\rho(\phi)\mathbf{v}+\mathbf{J}))=\textnormal{div}\;\mathbf{T},

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{div}\;\mathbf{v}=0

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q,

(1.9a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\sigma(\psi)\mathbf{w})+{\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}}(\mathbf{w}\otimes(\sigma(\psi)\mathbf{w}+\mathbf{K}))={\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}}\mathbf{T}_{\Gamma}+\mathbf{Z},

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\;\mathbf{w}=0

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma,

(1.9b)

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}|_{\Gamma}=\mathbf{w},\hskip 20.00003pt\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{n}=0

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma,

(1.9c)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\phi+\textnormal{div}(\phi\mathbf{v})=\textnormal{div}(m_{\Omega}(\phi)\nabla\mu)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q,

(1.9d)

\displaystyle\mu=-\varepsilon\Delta\phi+\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}F^{\prime}(\phi)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q,

(1.9e)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\psi+\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(\psi\mathbf{w})=\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(m_{\Gamma}(\psi)\nabla_{\Gamma}\theta)-\beta m_{\Omega}(\phi)\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\mu

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma,

(1.9f)

\displaystyle\theta=-\delta\kappa\Delta_{\Gamma}\psi+\tfrac{1}{\delta}G^{\prime}(\psi)+\alpha\varepsilon\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\phi

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma,

(1.9g)

\displaystyle\varepsilon K\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\phi=\alpha\psi-\phi,\hskip 20.00003ptLm_{\Omega}(\phi)\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\mu=\beta\theta-\mu,

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002ptK,L\in[0,\infty],

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

(1.9h)

Here, viscous dynamics on the boundary are represented by an additional surface velocity field
\mathbf{w}:\Sigma\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
. In this model, we assume that the bulk and surface velocity fields are coupled through the trace relation (
1.9c
)
1
. This means that the motions of the bulk materials and the surface materials are directly related. Alternatively, stress-based coupling conditions between
\mathbf{v}
and
\mathbf{w}
might also be conceivable and could be discussed in future contributions.
As we demand the
non-penetration boundary condition
(
1.9c
)
2
, the trace relation (
1.9c
)
1
further entails that the surface velocity field
\mathbf{w}
is an evolving
tangential
vector field on
\Gamma
.
The location of the two materials in the bulk is represented by the bulk phase-field
\phi:Q\to\mathbb{R}
, whereas the two materials on the boundary are represented by the surface phase-field
\psi:\Sigma\to\mathbb{R}
.

The time evolution of
\mathbf{v}
and
\mathbf{w}
is described by the
bulk Navier–Stokes equation
(
1.9a
) and the
tangential surface Navier–Stokes equation
(
1.9b
), respectively. Both the flow in the bulk and the flow on the surface are assumed to be incompressible, that is, the velocity fields are divergence-free. Here,
\sigma(\psi)
denotes the phase-field dependent density on the surface, and is given by

\displaystyle\sigma(\psi)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{\tilde{\sigma}_{2}-\tilde{\sigma}_{1}}{2}\psi+\frac{\tilde{\sigma}_{2}+\tilde{\sigma}_{1}}{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

(1.10)

Similarly to the density in the bulk, the derivative of
\sigma
is constant. Therefore, we often write

\displaystyle\sigma^{\prime}\equiv\sigma^{\prime}(\psi)=\frac{\tilde{\sigma}_{2}-\tilde{\sigma}_{1}}{2}\,.

Furthermore,
\mathbf{T}_{\Gamma}
is a stress tensor, whereas
\mathbf{Z}
represents a surface force density that accounts for mechanical interactions between the bulk materials and the surface materials. They are given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{\Gamma}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbf{S}_{\Gamma}-q\mathbf{I}-\delta\kappa\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\otimes\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\hskip 10.00002pt\text{with}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathbf{S}_{\Gamma}\coloneqq 2\nu_{\Gamma}(\psi)\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma,

(1.11)

\displaystyle\mathbf{Z}

\displaystyle\coloneqq-\big[\mathbf{S}\,\mathbf{n}\big]_{\tau}+\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathbf{J}\cdot\mathbf{n})\mathbf{w}+\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathbf{J}_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbf{n})\mathbf{w}+\alpha\varepsilon\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\phi\,\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi-\gamma(\phi,\psi)\mathbf{w}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma,

(1.12)

The coefficient
\nu_{\Gamma}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
denotes the viscosity of the mixtures on the surface. As above,
\gamma(\phi,\psi)
denotes a slip coefficient associated with tangential friction. It may now depend on both phase-fields, as they are not necessarily coupled via the trace relation as in (
1.1h
). Moreover,

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\big((\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w})+(\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w})^{\top}\big)

denotes the
surface symmetric gradient
of
\mathbf{w}
. The quantity
\mathbf{K}
denotes a mass flux related to the interfacial motion on the boundary, while
\mathbf{J}_{\Gamma}
represents the mass flux corresponding to the transfer of material between bulk and surface. They are given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}

\displaystyle\coloneqq-\sigma^{\prime}\,\hskip 20.00003pt\mathbf{J}_{\Gamma}\coloneqq-\beta\rho^{\prime}\,

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

(1.13)

Employing the usual decompositions

\displaystyle-\varepsilon\textnormal{div}(\nabla\phi\otimes\nabla\phi)

\displaystyle=-\nabla\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\left|\nabla\phi\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}F(\phi)\right)+\mu\nabla\phi

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q,

\displaystyle-\delta\kappa\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\otimes\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi)

\displaystyle=-\nabla_{\Gamma}\left(\frac{\delta\kappa}{2}\left|\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{\delta}G(\psi)\right)+\theta\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi-\alpha\varepsilon\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\phi\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma,

and replacing the bulk and surface pressure by

\displaystyle\overline{p}

\displaystyle\coloneqq p+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\left|\nabla\phi\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}F(\phi)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q,

\displaystyle\overline{q}

\displaystyle\coloneqq q+\frac{\delta\kappa}{2}\left|\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{\delta}G(\psi)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma,

respectively, we can rewrite (
1.9a
)-(
1.9b
) as

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\rho(\phi)\mathbf{v})+\textnormal{div}(\mathbf{v}\otimes(\rho(\phi)\mathbf{v}+\mathbf{J}))-\textnormal{div}(2\nu_{\Omega}(\phi)\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v})+\nabla\overline{p}=\mu\nabla\phi

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q,

(1.14a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\sigma(\psi)\mathbf{w})+{\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}}(\mathbf{w}\otimes(\sigma(\psi)\mathbf{w}+\mathbf{K}))-{\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}}(2\nu_{\Gamma}(\psi)\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w})+\nabla_{\Gamma}\overline{q}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt=\theta\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi-2\nu_{\Omega}(\phi)\big[\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}\,\mathbf{n}\big]_{\tau}+\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathbf{J}\cdot\mathbf{n})\mathbf{w}+\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathbf{J}_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbf{n})\mathbf{w}-\gamma(\phi,\psi)\mathbf{w}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

(1.14b)

Moreover, for the subsequent analysis, it will prove useful to rewrite (
1.14
) in the so-called
non-conservative form

\displaystyle\rho(\phi)\partial_{t}\mathbf{v}+\rho(\phi)(\mathbf{v}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{v}+(\mathbf{J}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{v}-\textnormal{div}(2\nu_{\Omega}(\phi)\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v})+\nabla\overline{p}=\mu\nabla\phi

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q,

(1.15a)

\displaystyle\sigma(\psi)\partial_{t}\mathbf{w}+\sigma(\psi)(\mathbf{w}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma})\mathbf{w}+(\mathbf{K}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma})\mathbf{w}-{\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}}(2\nu_{\Gamma}(\psi)\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w})+\nabla_{\Gamma}\overline{q}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt=\theta\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi-2\nu_{\Omega}(\phi)\big[\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}\,\mathbf{n}\big]_{\tau}+\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathbf{J}\cdot\mathbf{n})\mathbf{w}-\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathbf{J}_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbf{n})\mathbf{w}-\gamma(\phi,\psi)\mathbf{w}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

(1.15b)

The energy functional associated with system (
1.9
) reads as

\displaystyle\begin{split}E(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w},\phi,\psi)&=\int_{\Omega}\frac{\rho(\phi)}{2}\left|\mathbf{v}\right|^{2}\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}\frac{\sigma(\psi)}{2}\left|\mathbf{w}\right|^{2}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma+\int_{\Omega}\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\left|\nabla\phi\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}F(\phi)\;\mathrm{d}x\\
&\quad+\int_{\Gamma}\frac{\delta\kappa}{2}\left|\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{\delta}G(\psi)+\frac{1}{2}\chi(K)(\alpha\psi-\phi)^{2}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma.\end{split}

(1.16)

Here, the function

\displaystyle\chi:[0,\infty]\rightarrow[0,\infty),\hskip 10.00002pt\chi(r)\coloneqq\begin{cases}r^{-1},&\text{if\penalty\ }r\in(0,\infty),\\
0,&\text{if\penalty\ }r\in\{0,\infty\},\end{cases}

(1.17)

is used to distinguish the different cases of the parameters
K
and
L
. Sufficiently regular solutions to system (
1.9
) satisfy the
energy dissipation law

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}E(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w},\phi,\psi)+\int_{\Omega}2\nu_{\Omega}(\phi)\left|\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}\right|^{2}\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}2\nu_{\Gamma}(\psi)\left|\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}\right|^{2}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma+\int_{\Gamma}\gamma(\phi,\psi)\left|\mathbf{w}\right|^{2}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma

(1.18)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\int_{\Omega}m_{\Omega}(\phi)\left|\nabla\mu\right|^{2}\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}m_{\Gamma}(\psi)\left|\nabla_{\Gamma}\theta\right|^{2}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma+\chi(L)\int_{\Gamma}(\beta\theta-\mu)^{2}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma=0

on
[0,T]

as well as the
mass conservation law

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\beta\int_{\Omega}\phi(t)\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}\psi(t)\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma=\beta\int_{\Omega}\phi(0)\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}\psi(0)\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma,&\textnormal{if\penalty\ }L\in[0,\infty),\\
\int_{\Omega}\phi(t)\;\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\Omega}\phi(0)\;\mathrm{d}x\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\int_{\Gamma}\psi(t)\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma=\int_{\Gamma}\psi(0)\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma,&\textnormal{if\penalty\ }L=\infty,\end{cases}

(1.19)

for all
t\in[0,T]
. This means that the mass is conserved separately in
\Omega
and
\Gamma
if
L=\infty
, while in the case
L\in[0,\infty)
, a transfer of material between bulk and surface is expected to occur (cf.
[
50
]
).

For prescribed velocity fields
\mathbf{v}
and
\mathbf{w}
, the convective bulk-surface Cahn–Hilliard subsystem (
1.9d
)-(
1.9g
) subject to the coupling conditions (
1.9h
) with
K,L\in[0,\infty]
was first analyzed in
[
53
]
in the case of regular potentials, and then in
[
54
]
and
[
39
]
for singular potentials.
The
non-convective
version of (
1.9d
)-(
1.9g
) (i.e.,
\mathbf{v}\equiv 0
and
\mathbf{w}\equiv 0
) has been the subject of extensive investigation in the literature. We refer to
[
20
,
29
,
33
,
34
,
41
,
63
,
56
,
61
,
59
,
60
,
75
]

for a selection of representative contributions. For comprehensive overviews on the Cahn–Hilliard equation with classical or dynamic boundary conditions, we recommend the recent review paper
[
79
]
as well as the book
[
62
]
.

1.4  Strategy of the mathematical analysis and plan of the paper

Our main result (Theorem
4.2
) establishes the existence of a global-in-time weak solution to (
1.9
) for arbitrary coupling parameters
K,L\in[0,\infty]
in both two and three dimensions. Since the convective bulk-surface Cahn–Hilliard subsystem has already been investigated in detail (see
[
54
,
39
]
), the central idea of our proof is to employ a semi-Galerkin scheme, in which the velocity fields are approximated on suitable finite-dimensional subspaces. For standard boundary conditions, such as no-slip or Navier-slip boundary conditions, this is typically achieved through the use of eigenfunctions of the Stokes operator subject to the corresponding boundary condition. However, in the present setting, the novel setting of dynamic boundary conditions (
1.9b
)-(
1.9c
) (especially the surface divergence condition (
1.9b
)
2
) imposed on the velocity field renders these eigenfunctions unsuitable. To overcome this difficulty, we introduce and analyze a related bulk-surface Stokes system along with the corresponding bulk-surface Stokes operator. By means of spectral theory, we show that this operator admits a countable family of eigenvalues with associated eigenfunctions, which in turn serve as a natural basis for approximating the velocity fields
\mathbf{v}
and
\mathbf{w}
.

This argument, however, can only be carried out in the case
L\in(0,\infty]
, since if
L=0
, admissible test functions are required to satisfy a specific trace relation. To address this issue, we establish the existence of weak solutions for
L=0
by considering the asymptotic limit
L\rightarrow 0
of weak solutions associated with
L>0
. However, in the case
L=0
, we require the densities of the bulk and surface materials to satisfy the compatibility condition

\displaystyle\beta(\tilde{\sigma}_{2}-\tilde{\sigma}_{1})=-(\tilde{\rho}_{2}-\tilde{\rho}_{1}).

(1.20)

It arises because our boundary condition on
\mathbf{v}
involves not only the flux term
\mathbf{J}\cdot\mathbf{n}
, but also the surface flux term
\mathbf{J}_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbf{n}
(see (
1.12
)). Note that while for the system (
1.1
) from
[
38
]
the term
\mathbf{J}\cdot\mathbf{n}
led to the requirement of matched densities (i.e.,
\tilde{\rho}_{1}=\tilde{\rho}_{2}
) if
L=0
(see
[
32
]
), our compatibility condition (
1.20
) is more general and even allows for unmatched densities in the case
L=0
.

The remainder of this paper is structured as follows. In Section
2
, we present all the necessary notation and preliminaries. Then, Section
3
is devoted to the derivation of the bulk-surface Navier–Stokes–Cahn–Hilliard model (
1.9
), which is carried out by means of local mass balance laws and local energy dissipation laws (both in the bulk and on the surface) as well as the Lagrange multiplier approach. Afterwards, in Section
4
, we introduce the notion of a weak solution to (
1.9
) and state our main result regarding the existence of a global-in-time weak solution. In Section
5
, we study a new bulk-surface Stokes system together with its associated bulk-surface Stokes operator. Section
6
is dedicated to the construction of a weak solution in the case
L\in(0,\infty]
. Lastly, in Section
7
, we investigate the asymptotic limit
L\rightarrow 0
, which proves the existence of a weak solution to (
1.9
) in the limit case
L=0
.

2  Notation and preliminaries

2.1  General notation and function spaces

We write
\mathbb{N}
to denote the set of natural numbers (excluding zero). For any real Banach space
X
with
\|\cdot\|_{X}
, its dual space is denoted by
X^{\prime}
. The corresponding duality pairing of elements
\phi\in X^{\prime}
and
\zeta\in X
is denoted by
\langle\phi,\zeta\rangle_{X}
. If
X
is a Hilbert space, we write
\left(\cdot,\cdot\right)_{X}
to denote its inner product. Given
p\in[1,\infty]
and an interval
I\subset[0,\infty)
, the Bochner space
L^{p}(I,X)
consists of all Bochner measurable,
p
-integrable functions defined on
I
with values in
X
. We further define
W^{1,p}(I;X)
as the space of all functions
f\in L^{p}(I;X)
with vector-valued distributional time derivative
\partial_{t}f\in L^{p}(I;X)
. In particular, we set
H^{1}(I;X)=W^{1,2}(I;X)
. The set of continuous functions
f:I\rightarrow X
is denoted by
C(I;X)
. Moreover,
C_{w}(I;X)
denotes the space of functions
f:I\to X
which are continuous on
I
with respect to the weak topology on
X
, i.e., the map
I\ni t\mapsto\langle\phi,f(t)\rangle_{X}
is continuous for all
\phi\in X^{\prime}
.
At this point, we also recall the following lemma, which can be found in
[
1
, Lemma 4.1]
.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
I\subset\mathbb{R}
be a compact interval, and let
X,Y
be Banach spaces with
Y\hookrightarrow X
and
X^{\prime}\hookrightarrow Y^{\prime}
densely. Then the continuous embedding
L^{\infty}(I;Y)\cap C(I;X)\hookrightarrow C_{w}(I;Y)
holds.

In the remainder of this section, let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
d=2,3
, be a bounded domain with sufficiently smooth boundary
\Gamma\coloneqq\partial\Omega
, and let
\mathbf{n}
denote the exterior unit normal vector field on
\Gamma
. For any
s\geq 0
and
p\in[1,\infty]
, the Lebesgue and Sobolev–Slobodeckij spaces for functions mapping from
\Omega
to
\mathbb{R}
are denoted as
L^{p}(\Omega)
and
W^{s,p}(\Omega)
, respectively. We write
\|\cdot\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}
and
\|\cdot\|_{W^{s,p}(\Omega)}
to denote the standard norms on these spaces. If
p=2
, we use the notation
H^{s}(\Omega)=W^{s,2}(\Omega)
, with the convention that
H^{0}(\Omega)
is identified with
L^{2}(\Omega)
. For the Lebesgue and Sobolev–Slobodeckij spaces on the boundary
\Gamma
, we use an analogous notation. The corresponding spaces of vector-valued mapping into
\mathbb{R}^{d}
or matrix-valued functions mapping into
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
are denoted by boldface letters, namely
\mathbf{L}^{p}
,
\mathbf{W}^{s,p}
and
\mathbf{H}^{s}
. In particular, we write
\mathbf{L}^{p}_{\tau}(\Gamma)
,
\mathbf{W}^{s,p}_{\tau}(\Gamma)
and
\mathbf{H}^{s}_{\tau}(\Gamma)
to denote the corresponding spaces of tangential vector fields on
\Gamma
.

Moreover, for
p\in[1,\infty]
, we further introduce the first-order homogeneous Sobolev spaces

\displaystyle\dot{W}^{1,p}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\big\{u\in L^{p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)\,:\,\nabla u\in\mathbf{L}^{p}(\Omega)\big\}\big/_{\sim}\,,

\displaystyle\dot{W}^{1,p}(\Gamma)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\big\{v\in L^{p}(\Gamma)\,:\,\nabla_{\Gamma}v\in\mathbf{L}^{p}(\Gamma)\big\}\big/_{\sim}\,.

Here,
L^{p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
is the collection of all functions, which belong to
L^{p}(K)
for every compact subset
K\subset\Omega
. On the boundary, we simply consider
L^{p}(\Gamma)
, since
\Gamma
is itself a compact submanifold.
Moreover, the notation
/_{\sim}\,
indicates that functions differing only by an additive constant are considered equivalent and thus identified with each other. Consequently, the functionals
{\|\nabla\,\cdot\|_{\mathbf{L}^{p}(\Omega)}}
and
{\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\,\cdot\|_{\mathbf{L}^{p}(\Gamma)}}
define norms on the spaces
\dot{W}^{1,p}(\Omega)
and
\dot{W}^{1,p}(\Gamma)
, respectively.

Furthermore, we write
\mathbf{P}^{\Gamma}
to denote the orthogonal projection of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
into the tangent space of the submanifold
\Gamma
.
This means that
\mathbf{P}^{\Gamma}
is a linear map, which can be represented as

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}^{\Gamma}=\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{n}\otimes\mathbf{n},

where
\mathbf{I}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is the unity matrix.
For any vector field
\mathbf{w}
on
\Gamma
, we also use the notation

\displaystyle[\mathbf{w}]_{\tau}=\mathbf{P}^{\Gamma}(\mathbf{w}).

2.2  Differential operators in the bulk and on the surface

Let
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
and
g:\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}
be scalar functions, let
\mathbf{v}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mathbf{w}:\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be vector fields, and let
\mathbf{A}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and
\mathbf{B}:\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
be matrix-valued functions.
For now, we assume that the boundary
\Gamma
and all these functions are sufficiently regular.
As usual,
\nabla f
,
\textnormal{div}\,\mathbf{v}
, and
\Delta f=\textnormal{div}(\nabla f)
denote the gradient of
f
, the divergence of
\mathbf{v}
and the Laplacian of
f
.
Analogously,
\nabla_{\Gamma}g
,
\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\,\mathbf{w}
, and
\Delta_{\Gamma}g=\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(\nabla_{\Gamma}g)
are the tangential gradient of
g
, the surface divergence of
\mathbf{w}
and the Laplace–Beltrami operator applied to
g
.
We point out that
\nabla f
and
\nabla_{\Gamma}g
are to be understood as column vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
For bulk vector fields, the gradient is defined as

\displaystyle\nabla\mathbf{v}=\begin{pmatrix}(\nabla\mathbf{v}_{1})^{T}\\
\vdots\\
(\nabla\mathbf{v}_{d})^{T}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d},

where
\mathbf{v}_{1},...,\mathbf{v}_{d}
denote the components of
\mathbf{v}
. In other words,
\nabla\mathbf{v}
is the Jacobian of
\mathbf{v}
. On the surface
\Gamma
, the projective (covariant) gradient of
\mathbf{w}
is defined as

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{P}^{\Gamma}\nabla\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}\mathbf{P}^{\Gamma},

where
\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}
is an extension of
\mathbf{w}
to an open neighborhood of
\Gamma
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. However, the expression
\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}
does not depend on the concrete choice of this extension. If
\mathbf{w}
is a tangential vector field, its gradient can also be expressed as

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{P}^{\Gamma}\begin{pmatrix}(\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}_{1})^{T}\\
\vdots\\
(\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}_{d})^{T}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d},

where
\mathbf{w}_{1},...,\mathbf{w}_{d}
denote the components of
\mathbf{w}
.
For the matrix-valued functions
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
, we further define the divergences

\displaystyle\textnormal{div}\,\mathbf{A}=\begin{pmatrix}\textnormal{div}\,\mathbf{A}_{1\ast}\\
\vdots\\
\textnormal{div}\,\mathbf{A}_{d\ast}\end{pmatrix},\hskip 20.00003pt\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\,\mathbf{B}=\mathbf{P}^{\Gamma}\begin{pmatrix}\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\,\mathbf{B}_{1\ast}\\
\vdots\\
\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\,\mathbf{B}_{d\ast}\end{pmatrix}.

Here,
\mathbf{A}_{1\ast},...,\mathbf{A}_{d\ast}
and
\mathbf{B}_{1\ast},...,\mathbf{B}_{d\ast}
denote the rows of
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
, respectively.

2.3  Bulk-surface product spaces

For any
s\geq 0
and
p\in[1,\infty]
, we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{p}\coloneqq L^{p}(\Omega)\times L^{p}(\Gamma),\hskip 20.00003pt\mathcal{W}^{s,p}\coloneqq W^{s,p}(\Omega)\times W^{s,p}(\Gamma).

We write
\mathcal{H}^{s}=\mathcal{W}^{s,2}
and identify
\mathcal{L}^{2}
with
\mathcal{H}^{0}
. We point out that
\mathcal{H}^{s}
is a Hilbert space with respect to the inner product

\displaystyle\big(\left(\phi,\psi\right),\left(\zeta,\xi\right)\big)_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}\coloneqq\left(\phi,\zeta\right)_{H^{s}(\Omega)}+\left(\psi,\xi\right)_{H^{s}(\Gamma)}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all\penalty\ }(\phi,\psi),(\zeta,\xi)\in\mathcal{H}^{s},

and its induced norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}\coloneqq\left(\cdot,\cdot\right)_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}^{1/2}
. We further recall that the duality pairing on
\mathcal{H}^{1}
satisfies

\displaystyle\big<\left(\phi,\psi\right),\left(\zeta,\xi\right)\big>_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}=\left(\phi,\zeta\right)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\left(\psi,\xi\right)_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}

for all
(\zeta,\xi)\in\mathcal{H}^{1}
if
(\phi,\psi)\in\mathcal{L}^{2}
.

For every
\phi\in H^{1}(\Omega)^{\prime}
, we denote by
{\langle\phi\rangle}_{\Omega}=\left|\Omega\right|^{-1}\langle\phi,1\rangle_{H^{1}(\Omega)}
its generalized mean value over
\Omega
. If
\phi\in L^{1}(\Omega)
, its spatial mean can simply be expressed as
{\langle\phi\rangle}_{\Omega}=\left|\Omega\right|^{-1}\int_{\Omega}\phi\;\mathrm{d}x
. The spatial mean of any
\psi\in H^{1}(\Gamma)^{\prime}
, denoted by
{\langle\psi\rangle}_{\Gamma}
, is defined similarly. We further define for any
p\in[2,\infty]
the space

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{p}_{(0)}\coloneqq L^{p}_{(0)}(\Omega)\times L^{p}_{(0)}(\Gamma),

where

\displaystyle L^{p}_{(0)}(\Omega)\coloneqq\big\{\phi\in L^{p}(\Omega):{\langle\phi\rangle}_{\Omega}=0\big\},\hskip 20.00003ptL^{p}_{(0)}(\Gamma)\coloneqq\big\{\psi\in L^{p}(\Gamma):{\langle\psi\rangle}_{\Gamma}=0\big\}.

Now, let
L\in[0,\infty]
and
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
. We introduce the linear subspace

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\mathcal{H}^{1},&\text{if\penalty\ }L\in(0,\infty],\\
\big\{(\phi,\psi)\in\mathcal{H}^{1}:\phi=\beta\psi\text{\penalty\ a.e.\penalty\ on\penalty\ }\Gamma\big\},&\text{if\penalty\ }L=0.\end{cases}

Subject to the inner product
\left(\cdot,\cdot\right)_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta}}\coloneqq\left(\cdot,\cdot\right)_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}
and its induced norm,
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta}
is a Hilbert space. Furthermore, we define the product

\displaystyle\big<\left(\phi,\psi\right),\left(\zeta,\xi\right)\big>_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta}}=\left(\phi,\zeta\right)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\left(\psi,\xi\right)_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}

for all
(\phi,\psi),(\zeta,\xi)\in\mathcal{L}^{2}
. Using the Riesz representation theorem, this product can be extended to a duality pairing on
(\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta})^{\prime}\times\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta}
, which will also be denoted as
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta}}
.

Next, for
(\phi,\psi)\in(\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta})^{\prime}
, we define the generalized bulk-surface mean

\displaystyle\textnormal{mean}\left(\phi,\psi\right)\coloneqq\frac{\big<\left(\phi,\psi\right),\left(\beta,1\right)\big>_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta}}}{\beta^{2}\left|\Omega\right|+\left|\Gamma\right|},

which reduces to

\displaystyle\textnormal{mean}\left(\phi,\psi\right)=\frac{\beta\left|\Omega\right|{\langle\phi\rangle}_{\Omega}+\left|\Gamma\right|{\langle\psi\rangle}_{\Gamma}}{\beta^{2}\left|\Omega\right|+\left|\Gamma\right|}

if
(\phi,\psi)\in\mathcal{L}^{2}
. We then define the closed linear subspace

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{1}_{L,\beta}=\begin{cases}\{(\phi,\psi)\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta}:\textnormal{mean}\left(\phi,\psi\right)=0\},&\text{if\penalty\ }L\in[0,\infty),\\
\{(\phi,\psi)\in\mathcal{H}^{1}:{\langle\phi\rangle}_{\Omega}={\langle\psi\rangle}_{\Gamma}=0\},&\text{if\penalty\ }L=\infty.\end{cases}

Note that
\mathcal{V}^{1}_{L,\beta}
is a Hilbert spaces with respect to the inner product
\left(\cdot,\cdot\right)_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}
. We further introduce the bilinear form

\displaystyle\big(\left(\phi,\psi\right),\left(\zeta,\xi\right)\big)_{L,\beta}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\nabla\phi\cdot\nabla\zeta\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\xi\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\chi(L)\int_{\Gamma}(\beta\psi-\phi)(\beta\xi-\zeta)\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma

for all
(\phi,\psi),(\zeta,\xi)\in\mathcal{H}^{1}
, where

\displaystyle\chi:[0,\infty]\rightarrow[0,\infty),\hskip 20.00003pt\chi(r)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\frac{1}{r},&\text{if\penalty\ }r\in(0,\infty),\\
0,&\text{if\penalty\ }r\in\{0,\infty\}.\end{cases}

Moreover, we define

\displaystyle\|(\phi,\psi)\|_{L,\beta}\coloneqq\big(\left(\phi,\psi\right),\left(\phi,\psi\right)\big)_{L,\beta}^{1/2}

for all
\left(\phi,\psi\right)\in\mathcal{H}^{1}
. The bilinear form defines an inner product on
\mathcal{V}^{1}_{L,\beta}
, and
\|\cdot\|_{L,\beta}
defines a norm on
\mathcal{V}^{1}_{L,\beta}
, that is equivalent to the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}
, see, e.g.,
[
51
, Corollary A.2]
. In particular,
(\mathcal{V}^{1}_{L,\beta},\left(\cdot,\cdot\right)_{L,\beta},\|\cdot\|_{L,\beta})
is a Hilbert space. Next, we define the spaces

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{-1}_{L,\beta}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\{(\phi,\psi)\in(\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta})^{\prime}:\textnormal{mean}\left(\phi,\psi\right)=0\},&\text{if\penalty\ }L\in[0,\infty),\\
\{(\phi,\psi)\in(\mathcal{H}^{1})^{\prime}:{\langle\phi\rangle}_{\Omega}={\langle\psi\rangle}_{\Gamma}=0\},&\text{if\penalty\ }L=\infty.\end{cases}

Now, using the Lax–Milgram theorem, we infer that for any
(\phi,\psi)\in\mathcal{V}^{-1}_{L,\beta}
, there exists a unique weak solution
\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}(\phi,\psi)=(\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}^{\Omega}(\phi,\psi),\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}^{\Gamma}(\phi,\psi))\in\mathcal{V}_{L,\beta}^{1}
to the elliptic boundary value problem

\displaystyle-\Delta_{\Gamma}\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}^{\Omega}

\displaystyle=-\phi,

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in\penalty\ }\Omega,

\displaystyle-\Delta_{\Gamma}\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}^{\Gamma}+\beta\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}^{\Omega}

\displaystyle=-\psi,

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Gamma,

\displaystyle L\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}^{\Omega}

\displaystyle=\beta\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}^{\Gamma}-\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}^{\Omega},

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Gamma.

To be precise,
\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}(\phi,\psi)
satisfies the weak formulation

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}(\phi,\psi),\left(\zeta,\xi\right)\big)_{L,\beta}=-\big<\left(\phi,\psi\right),\left(\zeta,\xi\right)\big>_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta}}

for all
(\zeta,\xi)\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta}
. We directly infer that

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}(\phi,\psi)\|_{L,\beta}\leq C\|(\phi,\psi)\|_{(\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta})^{\prime}}

for a constant
C>0
that is independent of
(\phi,\psi)
.
In this way, we can define the solution operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}:\mathcal{V}_{L,\beta}^{-1}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}_{L,\beta}^{1},\hskip 20.00003pt(\phi,\psi)\mapsto\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}(\phi,\psi)=(\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}^{\Omega}(\phi,\psi),\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}^{\Gamma}(\phi,\psi)).

Furthermore, an inner product and its induced norm on
\mathcal{V}_{L,\beta}^{-1}
are given by

\displaystyle\big(\left(\phi,\psi\right),\left(\zeta,\xi\right)\big)_{L,\beta,\ast}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\big(\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}(\phi,\psi),\mathcal{S}_{L,\beta}(\zeta,\xi)\big)_{L,\beta},

\displaystyle\|(\phi,\psi)\|_{L,\beta,\ast}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\big(\left(\phi,\psi\right),\left(\phi,\psi\right)\big)_{L,\beta,\ast}^{1/2},

for
(\phi,\psi),(\zeta,\xi)\in\mathcal{V}_{L,\beta}^{-1}
. This norm is equivalent to the standard norm
\|\cdot\|_{(\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta})^{\prime}}
on
\mathcal{V}_{L,\beta}^{-1}
. For the case
L\in(0,\infty)
, we refer to
[
51
, Theorem 3.3 and Corollary 3.5]
for a proof of these statements. In the other cases, these results can be proven analogously. We further recall the following bulk-surface Poincaré inequality, which was established in
[
51
, Lemma A.1]
.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
K\in[0,\infty)
and
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
with
\alpha\beta\left|\Omega\right|+\left|\Gamma\right|\neq 0
. Then there exists a constant
C_{P}>0
depending only on
K,\alpha,\beta
and
\Omega
such that

\displaystyle\|(\phi,\psi)\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\leq C_{P}\|(\phi,\psi)\|_{K,\alpha}

for all pairs
(\phi,\psi)\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{K,\alpha}
satisfying
\textnormal{mean}\left(\phi,\psi\right)=0
.

2.4  Spaces of tangential and divergence-free vector fields

For
p\in[2,\infty]
, we introduce the spaces

\displaystyle\mathbf{L}^{p}_{\textnormal{div}}(\Omega)\coloneqq\{\mathbf{v}\in\mathbf{L}^{p}(\Omega):\textnormal{div}\,\mathbf{v}=0\ \text{in\penalty\ }\Omega,\ \mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{n}=0\ \text{on\penalty\ }\Gamma\}\,,

\displaystyle\mathbf{L}^{p}_{\textnormal{div}}(\Gamma)\coloneqq\{\mathbf{w}\in\mathbf{L}^{p}(\Gamma):\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\,\mathbf{w}=0,\ \mathbf{w}\cdot\mathbf{n}=0\ \text{on\penalty\ }\Gamma\},

and we set

\displaystyle\mathbfcal{L}^{p}_{\textnormal{div}}\coloneqq\mathbf{L}^{p}_{\textnormal{div}}(\Omega)\times\mathbf{L}^{p}_{\textnormal{div}}(\Gamma).

Then, for
s\geq 0
and
p\in[2,\infty]
, we define
\mathbf{W}^{s,p}_{\textnormal{div}}(\Omega)=\mathbf{W}^{s,p}(\Omega)\cap\mathbf{L}^{2}_{\textnormal{div}}(\Omega)
. Analogously, we define
\mathbf{W}^{s,p}_{\textnormal{div}}(\Gamma)=\mathbf{W}^{s,p}(\Gamma)\cap\mathbf{L}^{2}_{\textnormal{div}}(\Gamma)
, and then set
\mathbfcal{W}^{s,p}_{\textnormal{div}}=\mathbf{W}^{s,p}_{\textnormal{div}}(\Omega)\times\mathbf{W}^{s,p}_{\textnormal{div}}(\Gamma)
. Furthermore, for
s>\frac{1}{2}
, we set

\displaystyle\mathbfcal{W}^{s,p}_{0}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\in\mathbfcal{W}^{s,p}:\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{n}=0,\ \mathbf{v}|_{\Gamma}=\mathbf{w}\ \text{on\penalty\ }\Gamma\}\,,

\displaystyle\mathbfcal{W}^{s,p}_{0,\textnormal{div}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbfcal{W}^{s,p}_{0}\cap\mathbfcal{W}^{s,p}_{\textnormal{div}}\,.

As before, we use the notation
\mathbfcal{H}^{s}_{0}=\mathbfcal{W}^{s,2}_{0}
as well as
\mathbfcal{H}^{s}_{0,\textnormal{div}}=\mathbfcal{W}^{s,2}_{0,\textnormal{div}}
.

3  Model derivation

In this section, we will derive the bulk-surface Navier–Stokes–Cahn–Hilliard system (
1.9
).

3.1  Local mass balance laws

We want to describe the time evolution of two fluids, indexed with
j=1,2
, in a (sufficiently smooth) domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
d\in\{2,3\}
on a time interval
[0,T]
with
T>0
.
We write
Q=\Omega\times(0,T)
and
\Sigma=\Gamma\times(0,T)
with
\Gamma=\partial\Omega
.
Each bulk material is assumed to have a constant individual density
\tilde{\rho}_{j}
,
j=1,2
, whereas each surface material has a constant individual density
\tilde{\sigma}_{j}
,
j=1,2
.
We further assume that the mass densities
\rho_{j}:Q\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
j=1,2
, of the two fluids in the bulk satisfy the following mass balance law:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho_{j}+\textnormal{div}\;\widehat{\mathbf{J}}_{j}=0,\hskip 10.00002ptj=1,2,\hskip 10.00002pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q.

(3.1)

Here,
\widehat{\mathbf{J}}_{j}
are the mass fluxes that correspond to the motion of the two materials in the bulk. In our model, we also want to allow for a transfer of material between bulk and surface, which is, for instance, needed to describe absorption processes. To this end, we additionally consider functions
\sigma_{j}:\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
j=1,2
, which represent the mass densities of the two fluids on the surface. In general, the materials on the surface might differ from those in the bulk, which is, for example, the case if the materials are transformed by chemical reactions occurring at the boundary. Therefore, we interpret the densities
\sigma_{j}
as independent functions that are not necessarily the traces of the densities
\rho_{j}
, respectively.
They are supposed to satisfy the following mass balance law on the boundary:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\sigma_{j}+\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\;\widehat{\mathbf{K}}_{j}=\widehat{\mathbf{J}}_{\Gamma,j}\cdot\mathbf{n},\hskip 10.00002ptj=1,2,\hskip 10.00002pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

(3.2)

Here,
\widehat{\mathbf{K}}_{j}
stands for the mass flux on the boundary, and the right-hand side
\widehat{\mathbf{J}}_{\Gamma,j}
describes the transfer of mass between bulk and surface.
This exchange of material is assumed to be balanced by the relation

\displaystyle\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{J}}_{\Gamma,1}}{\tilde{\sigma}_{1}}\cdot\mathbf{n}+\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{J}}_{\Gamma,2}}{\tilde{\sigma_{2}}}\cdot\mathbf{n}=0\hskip 10.00002pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

(3.3)

We next assume that the motion of our materials is described by individual velocity fields
\mathbf{v}_{j}:Q\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mathbf{w}_{j}:\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
j=1,2
in the bulk and on the surface, respectively. These can be expressed as

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{j}=\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{J}}_{j}}{\rho_{j}}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q,\hskip 20.00003pt\mathbf{w}_{j}=\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{K}}_{j}}{\sigma_{j}}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma,\hskip 20.00003ptj=1,2.

(3.4)

Consequently, the mass balance laws (
3.1
) and (
3.2
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho_{j}+\textnormal{div}(\rho_{j}\mathbf{v}_{j})

\displaystyle=0,\hskip 10.00002pt\hskip 10.00002pt

\displaystyle j=1,2,\hskip 10.00002pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q,

(3.5)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\sigma_{j}+\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(\sigma_{j}\mathbf{w}_{j})

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mathbf{J}}_{\Gamma,j}\cdot\mathbf{n},\hskip 10.00002pt

\displaystyle j=1,2,\hskip 10.00002pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

(3.6)

We now write
\phi_{j}:Q\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
\psi_{j}:\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
j=1,2
, to denote the volume fractions of the two fluids in the bulk and on the surface, respectively. Provided that each component has a constant density
\tilde{\rho}_{j}
and
\tilde{\sigma}_{j}
,
j=1,2
, respectively, the volume fractions can be identified as

\displaystyle\phi_{j}=\frac{\rho_{j}}{\tilde{\rho}_{j}}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q,\hskip 20.00003pt\psi_{j}=\frac{\sigma_{j}}{\tilde{\sigma}_{j}}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma,\hskip 20.00003ptj=1,2.

(3.7)

Assuming that the excess volume is zero, we have

\displaystyle\phi_{1}+\phi_{2}=1\hskip 10.00002pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q,\hskip 20.00003pt\psi_{1}+\psi_{2}=1\hskip 10.00002pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

(3.8)

We further define the order parameters

\displaystyle\phi=\phi_{2}-\phi_{1}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q,\hskip 20.00003pt\psi=\psi_{2}-\psi_{1}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

(3.9)

Furthermore, let
\mathbf{v}:Q\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mathbf{w}:\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
denote the volume-averaged velocity fields associated with the two fluids in the bulk and on the surface, respectively. By means of the order parameters, they can be expressed as

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}

\displaystyle=\phi_{1}\mathbf{v}_{1}+\phi_{2}\mathbf{v}_{2}\hskip 10.00002pt

\displaystyle\text{in\penalty\ }Q,

(3.10)

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}

\displaystyle=\psi_{1}\mathbf{w}_{1}+\psi_{2}\mathbf{w}_{2}\hskip 10.00002pt

\displaystyle\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

(3.11)

We assume that neither fluid can penetrate the boundary
\Gamma
, and that the velocity field on the boundary is an extension of the one in the bulk. This leads to the conditions

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}|_{\Gamma}=\mathbf{w},\hskip 20.00003pt\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{n}=0\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

(3.12)

As the second relation entails that
\mathbf{v}|_{\Gamma}
is a tangential vector field, the same holds for
\mathbf{w}
because of the first identity.
We now introduce

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}_{j}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mathbf{J}}_{j}-\rho_{j}\mathbf{v},\hskip 10.00002pt

\displaystyle j=1,2,\hskip 10.00002pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q,

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}_{\Gamma,j}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mathbf{J}}_{\Gamma,j}-\sigma_{j}\mathbf{w},\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle j=1,2,\hskip 10.00002pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma,

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}_{j}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mathbf{K}}_{j}-\sigma_{j}\mathbf{w}\hskip 10.00002pt

\displaystyle j=1,2,\hskip 10.00002pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma,

to denote the mass fluxes relative to the volume-averaged velocities. In view of (
3.12
), the mass balance equations (
3.5
) and (
3.6
) can thus be rewritten as

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho_{j}+\textnormal{div}(\rho_{j}\mathbf{v})+\textnormal{div}\;\mathbf{J}_{j}

\displaystyle=0,\hskip 10.00002pt

\displaystyle j=1,2,\hskip 10.00002pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q,

(3.13)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\sigma_{j}+\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(\sigma_{j}\mathbf{w})+\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\;\mathbf{K}_{j}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{J}_{\Gamma,j}\cdot\mathbf{n},\hskip 10.00002pt

\displaystyle j=1,2,\hskip 10.00002pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

(3.14)

We now define the total mass densities

\displaystyle\rho

\displaystyle=\rho_{1}+\rho_{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q,

(3.15)

\displaystyle\sigma

\displaystyle=\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

(3.16)

By means of (
3.13
) and (
3.14
), this leads to the relations

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho+\textnormal{div}(\rho\mathbf{v})+\textnormal{div}(\mathbf{J}_{1}+\mathbf{J}_{2})

\displaystyle=0\hskip 10.00002pt\hskip 10.00002pt

\displaystyle\text{in\penalty\ }Q,

(3.17)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\sigma+\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(\sigma\mathbf{w})+\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{K}_{1}+\mathbf{K}_{2})

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{J}_{\Gamma,1}+\mathbf{J}_{\Gamma,2})\cdot\mathbf{n}\hskip 10.00002pt\hskip 10.00002pt

\displaystyle\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

(3.18)

3.2  Local balance laws for the linear momentum

The next step is to consider the local balance laws for the linear momentum:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\rho\mathbf{v})+\textnormal{div}(\rho\mathbf{v}\otimes\mathbf{v})

\displaystyle=\textnormal{div}\;\tilde{\mathbf{T}}\hskip 10.00002pt

\displaystyle\text{in\penalty\ }Q,

(3.19)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\sigma\mathbf{w})+{\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}}(\sigma\mathbf{w}\otimes\mathbf{w})

\displaystyle={\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}}\tilde{\mathbf{T}}_{\Gamma}+\mathbf{Z}\hskip 10.00002pt

\displaystyle\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

(3.20)

Here,
\tilde{\mathbf{T}}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{T}}_{\Gamma}
are stress tensors that need to be specified later by means of constitutive assumptions, and
\mathbf{Z}
is the surface force density that accounts for mechanical interactions between bulk and surface materials. We point out that
\mathbf{Z}
is a tangential vector field.
In the following, we write

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}=\mathbf{J}_{1}+\mathbf{J}_{2}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q,\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 10.00002pt\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{K}_{1}+\mathbf{K}_{2},\hskip 10.00002pt\mathbf{J}_{\Gamma}=\mathbf{J}_{\Gamma,1}+\mathbf{J}_{\Gamma,2}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

(3.21)

By means of (
3.17
), the balance law (
3.19
) can be reformulated as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\rho(\partial_{t}\mathbf{v}+\mathbf{v}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{v})&=\textnormal{div}\;\tilde{\mathbf{T}}+(\textnormal{div}\;\mathbf{J})\mathbf{v}\\
&=\textnormal{div}(\tilde{\mathbf{T}}+\mathbf{v}\otimes\mathbf{J})-\nabla\mathbf{v}\;\mathbf{J}\quad\text{in\penalty\ }Q.\end{split}

(3.22)

Analogously, using (
3.18
) to reformulate (
3.20
), we get

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sigma(\partial_{t}\mathbf{w}+\mathbf{w}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w})={\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}}(\tilde{\mathbf{T}}_{\Gamma}+\mathbf{w}\otimes\mathbf{K})-\big[\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}\;\mathbf{K}\big]_{\tau}-(\mathbf{J}_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbf{n})\mathbf{w}+\mathbf{Z}\quad\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.\end{split}

(3.23)

Thus, defining the frame indifferent objective tensors

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}=\tilde{\mathbf{T}}+\mathbf{v}\otimes\mathbf{J}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q,\hskip 20.00003pt\mathbf{T}_{\Gamma}=\tilde{\mathbf{T}}_{\Gamma}+\mathbf{w}\otimes\mathbf{K}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma,

(3.24)

we can rewrite (
3.22
) and (
3.23
) as

\displaystyle\rho\partial_{t}\mathbf{v}+\nabla\mathbf{v}\,(\rho\mathbf{v}+\mathbf{J})

\displaystyle=\textnormal{div}\;\mathbf{T}\hskip 10.00002pt

\displaystyle\text{in\penalty\ }Q,

(3.25)

\displaystyle\sigma\partial_{t}\mathbf{w}+\big[\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}\,(\sigma\mathbf{w}+\mathbf{K})\big]_{\tau}

\displaystyle={\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}}\mathbf{T}_{\Gamma}-(\mathbf{J}_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbf{n})\mathbf{w}+\mathbf{Z}\hskip 10.00002pt

\displaystyle\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

(3.26)

Next, we multiply the equations (
3.5
) each by
1/\tilde{\rho}_{j}
,
j=1,2
, and add the resulting equations. Recalling (
3.7
), (
3.9
) and (
3.10
), we obtain

\displaystyle\textnormal{div}\;\mathbf{v}=\textnormal{div}\left(\frac{\rho_{1}}{\tilde{\rho}_{1}}\mathbf{v}_{1}+\frac{\rho_{2}}{\tilde{\rho}_{2}}\mathbf{v}_{2}\right)=-\partial_{t}\left(\frac{\rho_{1}}{\tilde{\rho}_{1}}+\frac{\rho_{2}}{\tilde{\rho}_{2}}\right)=-\partial_{t}1=0\hskip 10.00002pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q,

(3.27)

which means that the volume-averaged velocity field is divergence-free. Similarly, multiplying (
3.6
) by
1/\tilde{\sigma}_{j}
,
j=1,2
, adding the resulting equations, and recalling (
3.3
), (
3.7
), (
3.9
) and (
3.11
), we deduce

\displaystyle\begin{split}\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}&=\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\tilde{\sigma}_{1}}\mathbf{w}_{1}+\frac{\sigma_{2}}{\tilde{\sigma}_{2}}\mathbf{w}_{2}\right)\\
&=-\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\tilde{\sigma}_{1}}+\frac{\sigma_{2}}{\tilde{\sigma}_{2}}\right)+\left(\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{J}}_{\Gamma,1}}{\tilde{\sigma}_{1}}+\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{J}}_{\Gamma,2}}{\tilde{\sigma}_{2}}\right)\cdot\mathbf{n}=0\quad\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.\end{split}

(3.28)

We now define the quantities

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{J}}_{j}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbf{J}_{j}}{\tilde{\rho}_{j}},\hskip 10.00002pt

\displaystyle j=1,2,\hskip 20.00003pt\mathbf{J}_{\phi}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathbf{J}}_{2}-\tilde{\mathbf{J}}_{1}\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\text{in\penalty\ }Q,

(3.29)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{J}}_{\Gamma,j}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbf{J}_{\Gamma,j}}{\tilde{\sigma}_{j}},\hskip 10.00002pt

\displaystyle j=1,2,\hskip 20.00003pt\mathbf{J}_{\psi}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathbf{J}}_{\Gamma,2}-\tilde{\mathbf{J}}_{\Gamma,1}\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma,

(3.30)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{j}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbf{K}_{j}}{\tilde{\sigma}_{j}},\hskip 10.00002pt

\displaystyle j=1,2,\hskip 20.00003pt\mathbf{K}_{\psi}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{2}-\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{1}\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

(3.31)

Then, multiplying the equations (
3.13
) each by
1/\tilde{\rho}_{j}
,
j=1,2
, and subtracting the resulting equations, we deduce that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\phi+\textnormal{div}(\phi\mathbf{v})+\textnormal{div}\;\mathbf{J}_{\phi}=0\hskip 10.00002pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q.

(3.32)

Furthermore, multiplying the equations (
3.13
) each by
1/\tilde{\rho}_{j}
,
j=1,2
, adding the resulting equations, and recalling (
3.8
) and (
3.27
), we conclude that

\displaystyle\textnormal{div}(\tilde{\mathbf{J}}_{1}+\tilde{\mathbf{J}}_{2})=-\partial_{t}(\phi_{1}+\phi_{2})-\textnormal{div}((\phi_{1}+\phi_{2})\mathbf{v})=0\hskip 10.00002pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q.

(3.33)

Arguing similarly for (
3.14
), and recalling (
3.8
) and (
3.28
), we obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\psi+\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(\psi\mathbf{w})+\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\;\mathbf{K}_{\psi}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{J}_{\psi}\cdot\mathbf{n}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma,

(3.34)

and

\displaystyle\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{1}+\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{2})=-\partial_{t}(\psi_{1}+\psi_{2})-\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}((\psi_{1}+\psi_{2})\mathbf{w})+(\widehat{\mathbf{J}}_{\Gamma,1}+\widehat{\mathbf{J}}_{\Gamma,2})\cdot\mathbf{n}=0

(3.35)

on
\Sigma
. Using (
3.29
) and (
3.33
), we infer that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\textnormal{div}\;\mathbf{J}&=\frac{1}{2}\left[\tilde{\rho}_{2}\textnormal{div}\;\tilde{\mathbf{J}}_{2}+\tilde{\rho}_{1}\textnormal{div}\;\tilde{\mathbf{J}}_{1}\right]+\frac{1}{2}\left[\tilde{\rho}_{2}\textnormal{div}\;\tilde{\mathbf{J}}_{2}+\tilde{\rho}_{1}\textnormal{div}\;\tilde{\mathbf{J}}_{1}\right]\\
&=\frac{\tilde{\rho}_{2}-\tilde{\rho}_{1}}{2}\textnormal{div}\;\tilde{\mathbf{J}}_{2}-\frac{\tilde{\rho}_{2}-\tilde{\rho}_{1}}{2}\textnormal{div}\;\tilde{\mathbf{J}}_{2}=\frac{\tilde{\rho}_{2}-\tilde{\rho}_{1}}{2}\textnormal{div}\;\mathbf{J}_{\phi}\qquad\text{in\penalty\ }Q.\end{split}

(3.36)

Therefore, the fluxes
\mathbf{J}
and
\tfrac{1}{2}(\tilde{\rho}_{2}-\tilde{\rho}_{1})\mathbf{J}_{\phi}
can merely differ by an additive divergence-free function. As in
[
6
,
38
]
, we assume that

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}=\frac{\tilde{\rho}_{2}-\tilde{\rho}_{1}}{2}\mathbf{J}_{\phi}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q.

(3.37)

Proceeding analogously, we derive the identity

\displaystyle\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\;\mathbf{K}=\frac{\tilde{\sigma}_{2}-\tilde{\sigma}_{1}}{2}\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\;\mathbf{K}_{\psi}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma,

and assume that

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}=\frac{\tilde{\sigma}_{2}-\tilde{\sigma}_{1}}{2}\mathbf{K}_{\psi}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

(3.38)

Moreover, assuming that the phase-fields
\phi
and
\psi
only attain values in the interval
[-1,1]
, we use (
3.7
), (
3.8
), (
3.9
), (
3.15
) and (
3.16
) to conclude that the densities
\rho=\rho(\phi)
and
\sigma=\sigma(\psi)
are given by

\displaystyle\rho(\phi)

\displaystyle=\frac{\tilde{\rho}_{2}-\tilde{\rho}_{1}}{2}\phi+\frac{\tilde{\rho}_{2}+\tilde{\rho}_{1}}{2}\hskip 10.00002pt

\displaystyle\text{in\penalty\ }Q,

(3.39)

\displaystyle\sigma(\psi)

\displaystyle=\frac{\tilde{\sigma}_{2}-\tilde{\sigma}_{1}}{2}\psi+\frac{\tilde{\sigma}_{2}+\tilde{\sigma}_{1}}{2}\hskip 10.00002pt

\displaystyle\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

(3.40)

Finally, differentiating formula (
3.16
) with respect to time and employing (
3.34
) and (
3.38
), we deduce that
\sigma=\sigma(\psi)
fulfills the identity

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\sigma+\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(\sigma\mathbf{w})+\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\;\mathbf{K}

\displaystyle=\frac{\tilde{\sigma}_{2}-\tilde{\sigma}_{1}}{2}\mathbf{J}_{\psi}\cdot\mathbf{n}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

Comparing this equation with (
3.18
) (see also (
3.21
)), we finally conclude that

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbf{n}=\frac{\tilde{\sigma}_{2}-\tilde{\sigma}_{1}}{2}\mathbf{J}_{\psi}\cdot\mathbf{n}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

(3.41)

3.3  Local energy dissipation laws

Proceeding as in
[
6
]
and
[
38
]
, we consider the following energy density in the bulk:

\displaystyle e_{\Omega}(\mathbf{v},\phi,\nabla\phi)=\frac{\rho(\phi)}{2}\left|\mathbf{v}\right|^{2}+f(\phi,\nabla\phi)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q.

(3.42)

Here, the first summand on the right-hand side represents the kinetic energy density, while the second summand denotes the free energy density in the bulk. It is assumed to be of Ginzburg–Landau type, that is,

\displaystyle f(\phi,\nabla\phi)=\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\left|\nabla\phi\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}F(\phi)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q.

(3.43)

Moreover, we additionally introduce the following surface energy density

\displaystyle e_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{w},\phi,\psi,\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi)=\frac{\sigma(\psi)}{2}\left|\mathbf{w}\right|^{2}+g(\phi,\psi,\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

(3.44)

As in the bulk, the first summand on the right-hand side represents the kinetic energy density on the surface, whereas the second summand stands for the free energy density on the surface. It is also assumed to be of Ginzburg–Landau type (cf.
[
52
,
38
]
) with an additional term accounting for the relation between
\phi
and
\psi
, namely,

\displaystyle g(\phi,\psi,\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi)=\frac{\delta\kappa}{2}\left|\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{\delta}G(\psi)+\frac{1}{2}\chi(K)(\alpha\psi-\phi)^{2}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma,

(3.45)

for
K\in[0,\infty]
. Here,
\kappa\geq 0
acts as a weight for the surface Dirichlet energy density and
\chi
is the function defined in (
1.17
).

We now consider an arbitrary test volume
V(t)\subset\Omega
,
t\in[0,T]
, which is transported by the flow associated with
\mathbf{v}
. As we assume our physical system to be isothermal, the second law of thermodynamics leads to the dissipation inequality

\displaystyle\begin{split}0&\geq\left[\int_{V(t)}e_{\Omega}(\mathbf{v},\psi,\nabla\phi)\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\partial V(t)\cap\Gamma}e_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{w},\phi,\psi,\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi)\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma\right]\\
&\quad+\int_{\partial V(t)\cap\Omega}\mathbf{J}_{e}\cdot\mathbf{n}_{\partial V(t)}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma+\int_{\partial(\partial V(t)\cap\Gamma)}\mathbf{K}_{e}\cdot\mathbf{n}_{\partial(\partial V(t)\cap\Gamma)}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma_{\Gamma}.\end{split}

(3.46)

In this inequality,
\mathbf{J}_{e}
and
\mathbf{K}_{e}
are energy fluxes that will be specified later,
\mathbf{n}_{\partial V(t)}
denotes the outer unit normal vector field on
\partial V(t)
, whereas
\mathbf{n}_{\partial(\partial V(t)\cap\Omega)}
is the unit conormal vector field on
\partial(\partial V(t)\cap\Omega)
. Since we consider a thermodynamically closed system, there is no exchange of energy over the boundary
\Gamma
, and thus, the domain of the first integral in the second line is just
\partial V(t)\cap\Omega
instead of
\partial V(t)
. We further point out that
\partial(\partial V(t)\cap\Omega)\subset\Gamma
is to be understood as the relative boundary of the set
\partial V(t)\cap\Gamma
within the submanifold
\Gamma
. Applying Gauß’s divergence theorem on both integrals in the second line, and recalling that
\mathbf{n}_{\partial V(t)}=\mathbf{n}
on
\partial V(t)\cap\Gamma
, we reformulate (
3.46
) as

\displaystyle\begin{split}0&\geq\left[\int_{V(t)}e_{\Omega}(\mathbf{v},\psi,\nabla\phi)\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\partial V(t)\cap\Gamma}e_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{w},\phi,\psi,\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi)\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma\right]\\
&\quad+\int_{V(t)}\textnormal{div}\;\mathbf{J}_{e}\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\partial V(t)\cap\Gamma}\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\;\mathbf{K}_{e}-\mathbf{J}_{e}\cdot\mathbf{n}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma.\end{split}

(3.47)

Using the Reynolds transport theorem (see, e.g.,
[
12
, Theorem 2.11.1]
), we obtain the relation

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int_{V(t)}e_{\Omega}(\mathbf{v},\phi,\nabla\phi)\;\mathrm{d}x=\int_{V(t)}\partial_{t}e_{\Omega}(\mathbf{v},\phi,\nabla\phi)+\textnormal{div}(e_{\Omega}(\mathbf{v},\phi,\nabla\phi)\mathbf{v})\;\mathrm{d}x.

(3.48)

Similarly, applying the Reynolds transport theorem for evolving hypersurfaces (see, e.g.,
[
12
, Theorem 2.10.1]
), we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int_{\partial V(t)\cap\Gamma}e_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{w},\phi,\psi,\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi)\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma&=\int_{V(t)\cap\Gamma}\partial_{t}e_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{w},\phi,\psi,\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi)\\
&\qquad\qquad+\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(e_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{w},\phi,\psi,\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi)\mathbf{v})\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma.\end{split}

(3.49)

Combining (
3.47
), (
3.48
) and (
3.49
), we thus get

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\geq\int_{V(t)}\partial_{t}e_{\Omega}(\mathbf{v},\phi,\nabla\phi)+\textnormal{div}(e_{\Omega}(\mathbf{v},\phi,\nabla\phi)\mathbf{v})+\textnormal{div}\;\mathbf{J}_{e}\;\mathrm{d}x

(3.50)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\int_{\partial V(t)\cap\Gamma}\partial_{t}e_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{w},\phi,\psi,\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi)+\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(e_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{w},\phi,\psi,\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi)\mathbf{v})+\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\;\mathbf{K}_{e}-\mathbf{J}_{e}\cdot\mathbf{n}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma.

In particular, this inequality holds for all test volumes
V(t)\subset\Omega
with
\partial V(t)\cap\Gamma=\emptyset
. Therefore, we infer the local dissipation law in the bulk, which reads as

\displaystyle 0\geq-\mathcal{D}_{\Omega}\coloneqq\partial_{t}e_{\Omega}(\mathbf{v},\phi,\nabla\phi)+\textnormal{div}(e_{\Omega}(\mathbf{v},\phi,\nabla\phi)\mathbf{v})+\textnormal{div}\;\mathbf{J}_{e}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q.

(3.51)

Let now
c>0
be arbitrary, and consider a test volume
V(t)\subset\Omega
with
\left|V(t)\right|
being sufficiently small such that

\displaystyle\int_{V(t)}\mathcal{D}_{\Omega}\;\mathrm{d}x<c.

Then, invoking (
3.50
), we infer that

\displaystyle\int_{\partial V(t)\cap\Gamma}\partial_{t}e_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{w},\phi,\psi,\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi)+\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(e_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{w},\phi,\psi,\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi)\mathbf{v})+\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\;\mathbf{K}_{e}-\mathbf{J}_{e}\cdot\mathbf{n}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma<c.

As
c>0
and the test volume
V(t)
were arbitrary (except for the above smallness condition on
V(t)
), we conclude the following local dissipation law on the boundary:

\displaystyle 0\geq-\mathcal{D}_{\Gamma}\coloneqq\partial_{t}e_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{w},\phi,\psi,\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi)+\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(e_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{w},\phi,\psi,\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi)\mathbf{v})+\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\;\mathbf{K}_{e}-\mathbf{J}_{e}\cdot\mathbf{n}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

(3.52)

3.4  Completion of the model derivation via the Lagrange multiplier approach

We now want to identify the still undetermined flux terms using the
Lagrange multiplier approach
. Therefore, we introduce Lagrange multipliers
\mu
and
\theta
, which need to be determined in the course of this subsection. In the final model,
\mu
will represent the
bulk chemical potential
, while
\theta
will represent the
surface chemical potential
. Invoking the identities (
3.32
) and (
3.34
), the local energy dissipation laws (
3.51
) and (
3.52
) can be expressed as

\displaystyle 0\geq-\mathcal{D}_{\Omega}

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}e_{\Omega}(\mathbf{v},\phi,\nabla\phi)+\textnormal{div}(e_{\Omega}(\mathbf{v},\phi,\nabla\phi)\mathbf{v})+\textnormal{div}\;\mathbf{J}_{e}

\displaystyle-\mu(\partial_{t}\phi+\textnormal{div}(\phi\mathbf{v})+\textnormal{div}\;\mathbf{J}_{\phi})\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\text{in\penalty\ }Q,

(3.53)

\displaystyle 0\geq-\mathcal{D}_{\Gamma}

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}e_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{w},\phi,\psi,\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi)+\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(e_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{w},\phi,\psi,\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi)\mathbf{v})+\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\;\mathbf{K}_{e}

\displaystyle-\mathbf{J}_{e}\cdot\mathbf{n}-\theta(\partial_{t}\psi+\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(\psi\mathbf{w})+\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\;\mathbf{K}_{\psi}-\mathbf{J}_{\psi}\cdot\mathbf{n})\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

(3.54)

In the following, to provide a cleaner presentation, we will simply write
\rho
,
\sigma
,
f
and
g
instead of
\rho(\phi)
,
\sigma(\psi)
,
f(\phi,\nabla\phi)
and
g(\phi,\psi,\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi)
, respectively. Invoking the definition of the energy densities
e_{\Omega}
and
e_{\Gamma}
(see (
3.42
) and (
3.44
)), we reformulate (
3.53
) and (
3.54
) as

\displaystyle 0\geq-\mathcal{D}_{\Omega}

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}(\tfrac{1}{2}\rho\left|\mathbf{v}\right|^{2})+\textnormal{div}(\tfrac{1}{2}\rho\left|\mathbf{v}\right|^{2}\mathbf{v})+\partial_{t}f+\textnormal{div}(f\mathbf{v})+\textnormal{div}\;\mathbf{J}_{e}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt-\mu(\partial_{t}\phi+\textnormal{div}(\phi\mathbf{v})+\textnormal{div}\;\mathbf{J}_{\phi})

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q,

(3.55)

\displaystyle 0\geq-\mathcal{D}_{\Gamma}

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}(\tfrac{1}{2}\sigma\left|\mathbf{w}\right|^{2})+\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(\tfrac{1}{2}\sigma\left|\mathbf{w}\right|^{2}\mathbf{w})+\partial_{t}g+\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(g\mathbf{w})+\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\;\mathbf{K}_{e}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt-\mathbf{J}_{e}\cdot\mathbf{n}-\theta(\partial_{t}\psi+\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(\psi\mathbf{w})+\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\;\mathbf{K}_{\psi}-\mathbf{J}_{\psi}\cdot\mathbf{n})

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

(3.56)

In the following, for functions
h:Q\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
k:\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, their material derivatives are given by

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{\bullet}h

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}h+\mathbf{v}\cdot\nabla h\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\text{in\penalty\ }Q,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{\circ}k

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}k+\mathbf{w}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}k\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

We point out that in the material derivative
\partial_{t}^{\circ}
on the boundary, only the tangential gradient, instead of the full gradient, is relevant. This is because we already know from (
3.12
) that
\mathbf{w}
is a tangential vector field.
With these definitions at hand, we can express the derivatives
\partial_{t}^{\bullet}f
and
\partial_{t}^{\circ}g
through the chain rule as

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{\bullet}f

\displaystyle=\partial_{\phi}f\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\phi+\partial_{\nabla\phi}f\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\nabla\phi\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\text{in\penalty\ }Q,

(3.57)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{\circ}g

\displaystyle=\partial_{\phi}g\partial_{t}^{\circ}\phi+\partial_{\psi}g\partial_{t}^{\circ}\psi+\partial_{\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi}g\partial_{t}^{\circ}\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

(3.58)

3.4.1
Computations in the bulk

In the bulk we proceed exactly as in
[
6
]
and
[
38
]
to reformulate the inequality (
3.4
) as

\displaystyle\begin{split}0&\geq\textnormal{div}[\mathbf{J}_{e}-\tfrac{1}{2}\left|\mathbf{v}\right|^{2}\mathbf{J}+\mathbf{T}^{\top}\mathbf{v}-\mu\mathbf{J}_{\phi}+\partial_{\nabla\phi}f\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\phi]\\
&\quad+[\partial_{\phi}f-\textnormal{div}(\partial_{\nabla\phi}f)-\mu]\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\phi\\
&\quad-[\mathbf{T}+\nabla\phi\otimes\partial_{\nabla\phi}f]:\nabla\mathbf{v}+\nabla\mu\cdot\mathbf{J}_{\phi}\qquad\qquad\text{in\penalty\ }Q.\end{split}

(3.59)

To ensure that (
3.59
) holds, we now choose the chemical potential
\mu
and the energy flux
\mathbf{J}_{e}
as

\displaystyle\mu

\displaystyle=\partial_{\phi}f-\textnormal{div}(\partial_{\nabla\phi}f)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q,

(3.60)

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}_{e}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\left|\mathbf{v}\right|^{2}\mathbf{J}-\mathbf{T}^{\top}\mathbf{v}+\mu\mathbf{J}_{\phi}-\partial_{\nabla\phi}f\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\phi

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q.

(3.61)

In view of these choices, the first two lines of the right-hand side of (
3.59
) vanish. Furthermore, we assume the mass flux
\mathbf{J}_{\phi}
to be of Fick’s type, which means that

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}_{\phi}=-m_{\Omega}(\phi)\nabla\mu\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q.

(3.62)

Here,
m_{\Omega}=m_{\Omega}(\phi)
is a non-negative function, which represents the mobility in the bulk. Therefore, (
3.59
) reduces to

\displaystyle 0\geq-[\mathbf{T}+\nabla\phi\otimes\partial_{\nabla\phi}f]:\nabla\mathbf{v}-m_{\Omega}(\phi)\left|\nabla\mu\right|^{2}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q.

(3.63)

We next define the tensor

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}=\mathbf{T}+p\mathbf{I}+\nabla\phi\otimes\partial_{\nabla\phi}f\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q.

Here, the scalar variable
p
denotes the pressure in the bulk, and
\mathbf{I}
