stands for the identity. The tensor
\mathbf{S}
is the
viscous stress tensor
that corresponds to the irreversible changes of the energy caused by internal friction. For Newtonian fluids,
\mathbf{S}
is usually assumed to be given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}=2\nu_{\Omega}(\phi)\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v},

where the non-negative function
\nu_{\Omega}=\nu_{\Omega}(\phi)
represents the viscosity of the fluids in the bulk, and
\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}
is the symmetric gradient of
\mathbf{v}
. This choice, together with (
3.63
) and the identity
p\mathbf{I}:\nabla\mathbf{v}=p\,\textnormal{div}\;\mathbf{v}=0
in
Q
, implies that (
3.59
) is fulfilled as it reduces to

\displaystyle 0\geq-\nu_{\Omega}(\phi)\left|\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}\right|^{2}-m_{\Omega}(\phi)\left|\nabla\mu\right|^{2}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q.

(3.64)

In view of (
3.43
), the chemical potential
\mu
given by (
3.60
) can be expressed as

\displaystyle\mu=-\varepsilon\Delta\phi+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}F^{\prime}(\phi)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q.

(3.65)

Moreover, the total stress tensor
\mathbf{T}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{S}-p\mathbf{I}-\nabla\phi\otimes\partial_{\nabla\phi}f=2\nu_{\Omega}(\phi)\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}-p\mathbf{I}-\varepsilon\nabla\phi\otimes\nabla\phi\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q.

(3.66)

Furthermore, plugging (
3.62
) into (
3.32
), we obtain the equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\phi+\textnormal{div}(\phi\mathbf{v})=\textnormal{div}(m_{\Omega}(\phi)\nabla\mu)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q.

(3.67)

Moreover, (
3.19
) can be reformulated as

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\rho(\phi)\mathbf{v})+\textnormal{div}(\mathbf{v}\otimes(\rho(\phi)\mathbf{v}+\mathbf{J}))=\textnormal{div}\,\mathbf{T}

(3.68)

in
Q
, where
\mathbf{T}
is given by (
3.66
) and, due to (
3.37
) and (
3.62
), the flux term
\mathbf{J}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}=-\frac{\tilde{\rho}_{2}-\tilde{\rho}_{1}}{2}m_{\Omega}(\phi)\nabla\mu\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q.

(3.69)

3.4.2
Computations on the surface

Next, we consider the local energy dissipation law (
3.4
) on the surface. Recalling the formulas for
f
(see (
3.43
)) and
\mathbf{J}_{e}
(see (
3.61
)), we deduce from (
3.4
) that

\displaystyle\begin{split}0&\geq\partial_{t}(\tfrac{1}{2}\sigma\left|\mathbf{w}\right|^{2})+\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(\tfrac{1}{2}\sigma\left|\mathbf{w}\right|^{2}\mathbf{w})+\partial_{\phi}g\partial_{t}^{\circ}\phi+\partial_{\psi}g\partial_{t}^{\circ}\psi+\partial_{\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi}g\partial_{t}^{\circ}\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\\
&\quad+\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{K}_{e}-\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathbf{J}\cdot\mathbf{n})\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{T}\mathbf{n}\cdot\mathbf{v}-\mu\mathbf{J}_{\phi}\cdot\mathbf{n}+\varepsilon(\nabla\phi\cdot\mathbf{n})\partial_{t}^{\circ}\phi\\
&\quad-\theta\partial_{t}^{\circ}\psi-\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{K}_{\psi}\theta)+\nabla_{\Gamma}\theta\cdot\mathbf{K}_{\psi}+\theta\mathbf{J}_{\psi}\cdot\mathbf{n}\end{split}

(3.70)

on
\Sigma
. Here, we have used that

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}\cdot\nabla\phi=\mathbf{v}\cdot[\nabla_{\Gamma}\phi+\mathbf{n}(\nabla\phi\cdot\mathbf{n})]=\mathbf{w}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\phi\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma,

which follows from the definition of the surface gradient and the boundary condition (
3.12
).
Next, a straightforward computation yields

\displaystyle\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(\partial_{\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi}g\partial_{t}^{\circ}\psi)=\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(\partial_{\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi}g)\partial_{t}^{\circ}\psi+\partial_{\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi}g\cdot\partial_{t}^{\circ}\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi+[\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\otimes\partial_{\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi}g]:\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}

(3.71)

on
\Sigma
.
We now use this identity along with (
3.26
), (
3.34
), (
3.38
), (
3.16
) and (
3.41
) to reformulate (
3.70
). After a technical but straightforward computation, we arrive at

\displaystyle\begin{split}0&\geq\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}[\mathbf{K}_{e}-\tfrac{1}{2}\left|\mathbf{w}\right|^{2}\mathbf{K}+\mathbf{T}_{\Gamma}^{\top}\mathbf{w}-\theta\mathbf{K}_{\psi}+\partial_{\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi}g\partial_{t}^{\circ}\psi+\tfrac{1}{2}\chi(K)(\alpha\psi-\phi)^{2}\mathbf{w}]\\
&\quad+[\partial_{\phi}g+\varepsilon\nabla\phi\cdot\mathbf{n}]\partial_{t}^{\circ}\phi+[\partial_{\psi}g-\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(\partial_{\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi}g)-\theta]\partial_{t}^{\circ}\psi\\
&\quad+[[\mathbf{T}\mathbf{n}]_{\tau}-\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathbf{J}\cdot\mathbf{n})\mathbf{v}-\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathbf{J}_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbf{n})\mathbf{w}-\tfrac{1}{2}\chi(K)\nabla_{\Gamma}(\alpha\psi-\phi)^{2}+\mathbf{Z}]\cdot\mathbf{w}\\
&\quad-[\mathbf{T}_{\Gamma}+\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\otimes\partial_{\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi}g]:\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}+\nabla_{\Gamma}\theta\cdot\mathbf{K}_{\psi}+\theta\mathbf{J}_{\psi}\cdot\mathbf{n}-\mu\mathbf{J}_{\phi}\cdot\mathbf{n}\end{split}

(3.72)

on
\Sigma
.
In order to ensure that (
3.72
) is fulfilled, we choose the mass flux
\mathbf{K}_{\psi}
and the energy flux
\mathbf{K}_{e}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}_{\psi}

\displaystyle=-m_{\Gamma}(\psi)\nabla_{\Gamma}\theta

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma,

(3.73)

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}_{e}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left|\mathbf{w}\right|^{2}\mathbf{K}-\mathbf{T}_{\Gamma}^{\top}\mathbf{w}+\theta\mathbf{K}_{\psi}-\partial_{\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi}g\partial_{t}^{\circ}\psi-\tfrac{1}{2}\chi(K)(\alpha\psi-\phi)^{2}\mathbf{w}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

(3.74)

In (
3.73
),
m_{\Gamma}=m_{\Gamma}(\psi)
is a non-negative function representing the mobility on the surface. This choice of
\mathbf{K}_{e}
ensures that the first line of the right-hand side of (
3.72
) vanishes. Due to (
3.73
) and (
3.74
), inequality (
3.72
) reduces to

\displaystyle\begin{split}0&\geq[\partial_{\phi}g+\varepsilon\nabla\phi\cdot\mathbf{n}]\partial_{t}^{\circ}\phi+[\partial_{\psi}g-\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(\partial_{\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi}g)-\theta]\partial_{t}^{\circ}\psi\\
&\quad+[[\mathbf{T}\mathbf{n}]_{\tau}-\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathbf{J}\cdot\mathbf{n})\mathbf{v}-\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathbf{J}_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbf{n})\mathbf{w}-\tfrac{1}{2}\chi(K)\nabla_{\Gamma}(\alpha\psi-\phi)^{2}+\mathbf{Z}]\cdot\mathbf{w}\\
&\quad-[\mathbf{T}_{\Gamma}+\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\otimes\partial_{\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi}g]:\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}-m_{\Gamma}(\psi)\left|\nabla_{\Gamma}\theta\right|^{2}+\theta\mathbf{J}_{\psi}\cdot\mathbf{n}-\mu\mathbf{J}_{\phi}\cdot\mathbf{n}\end{split}

(3.75)

on
\Sigma
. We further assume that the flux terms
\mathbf{J}_{\phi}
and
\mathbf{J}_{\psi}
are directly proportional. To be precise, we suppose that there exists a real number
\beta
such that

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}_{\psi}\cdot\mathbf{n}=\beta\mathbf{J}_{\phi}\cdot\mathbf{n}=-\beta m_{\Omega}(\phi)\nabla\mu\cdot\mathbf{n}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

(3.76)

The parameter
\beta
is related to the transfer of material between bulk and surface and indicates the proportion of bulk material that becomes surface material (or vice versa).
Due to (
3.41
), this further implies that

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbf{n}=-\beta\frac{\tilde{\sigma}_{2}-\tilde{\sigma}_{1}}{2}m_{\Omega}(\phi)\nabla\mu\cdot\mathbf{n}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

(3.77)

The second equality in (
3.76
) follows from (
3.62
). We further make the constitutive assumption

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{Z}&=-[\mathbf{S}\mathbf{n}]_{\tau}+\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathbf{J}\cdot\mathbf{n})\mathbf{w}+\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathbf{J}_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbf{n})\mathbf{w}-\gamma(\phi,\psi)\mathbf{w}+\alpha\varepsilon(\nabla\phi\cdot\mathbf{n})\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\\
&=-[\mathbf{T}\mathbf{n}]_{\tau}+\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathbf{J}\cdot\mathbf{n})\mathbf{w}+\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathbf{J}_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbf{n})\mathbf{w}-\gamma(\phi,\psi)\mathbf{w}+\varepsilon(\nabla\phi\cdot\mathbf{n})\nabla_{\Gamma}(\alpha\psi-\phi)\end{split}

(3.78)

on
\Sigma
.
Here,
\gamma=\gamma(\phi,\psi)
is a non-negative slip parameter that is related to tangential friction at the boundary.
By this choice of
\mathbf{Z}
, inequality (
3.75
) further reduces to

\displaystyle\begin{split}0&\geq[\partial_{\phi}g+\varepsilon\nabla\phi\cdot\mathbf{n}]\partial_{t}^{\circ}\phi+[\partial_{\psi}g-\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(\partial_{\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi}g)-\theta]\partial_{t}^{\circ}\psi\\
&\quad+[\varepsilon(\nabla\phi\cdot\mathbf{n})-\chi(K)(\alpha\psi-\phi)]\,\nabla_{\Gamma}(\alpha\psi-\phi)\cdot\mathbf{w}\\
&\quad-[\mathbf{T}_{\Gamma}+\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\otimes\partial_{\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi}g]:\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}-m_{\Gamma}(\psi)\left|\nabla_{\Gamma}\theta\right|^{2}+\theta\mathbf{J}_{\psi}\cdot\mathbf{n}-\mu\mathbf{J}_{\phi}\cdot\mathbf{n}\end{split}

(3.79)

Analogously to the bulk, we introduce a tensor

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}_{\Gamma}=\mathbf{T}_{\Gamma}+q\mathbf{I}+\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\otimes\partial_{\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi}g\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

Here, the scalar variable
q
denotes the pressure on the surface, and the tensor
\mathbf{S}_{\Gamma}
is the
viscous stress tensor
on the surface, which is assumed to be given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}_{\Gamma}=2\nu_{\Gamma}(\psi)\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}.

(3.80)

Here,
\nu_{\Gamma}=\nu_{\Gamma}(\psi)
is a non-negative function representing the viscosity of the fluids on the surface and
\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}
is the symmetric surface gradient of
\mathbf{w}
. Therefore, the total stress tensor on the surface reads as

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{\Gamma}=\mathbf{S}_{\Gamma}-q\mathbf{I}-\delta\kappa\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\otimes\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

(3.81)

This means that (
3.20
) can be reformulated as

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\sigma(\psi)\mathbf{w})+{\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}}(\mathbf{w}\otimes(\sigma(\psi)\mathbf{w}+\mathbf{K}))={\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}}\mathbf{T}_{\Gamma}+\mathbf{Z}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{on $\Sigma$},

(3.82)

where, according to (
3.38
) and (
3.73
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}=-\frac{\tilde{\sigma}_{2}-\tilde{\sigma}_{1}}{2}m_{\Gamma}(\psi)\nabla_{\Gamma}\theta,

(3.83)

\mathbf{T}_{\Gamma}
is given by (
3.81
), and
\mathbf{Z}
is given by (
3.78
).
Moreover, by means of (
3.81
) and the identity
q\mathbf{I}:\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}=q\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\;\mathbf{w}=0
on
\Sigma
, (
3.75
) reduces to

\displaystyle\begin{split}0&\geq[\partial_{\phi}g+\varepsilon\nabla\phi\cdot\mathbf{n}]\partial_{t}^{\circ}\phi+[\partial_{\psi}g-\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(\partial_{\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi}g)-\theta]\partial_{t}^{\circ}\psi\\
&\quad+[\varepsilon(\nabla\phi\cdot\mathbf{n})-\chi(K)(\alpha\psi-\phi)]\,\nabla_{\Gamma}(\alpha\psi-\phi)\cdot\mathbf{w}\\
&\quad-\nu_{\Gamma}(\psi)\left|\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}\right|^{2}-\gamma(\phi,\psi)\left|\mathbf{w}\right|^{2}-m_{\Gamma}(\psi)\left|\nabla_{\Gamma}\theta\right|^{2}-(\beta\theta-\mu)m_{\Omega}(\phi)\nabla\mu\cdot\mathbf{n}\end{split}

(3.84)

on
\Sigma
. The first three terms in the third line of the right-hand side of (
3.84
) are clearly non-positive. The fourth term is non-positive if, depending on a parameter
L\in[0,\infty]
, one of the following boundary conditions holds:

\displaystyle\beta\theta-\mu

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on $\Sigma$ if $L=0$},

(3.85a)

\displaystyle m_{\Omega}(\phi)\nabla\mu\cdot\mathbf{n}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{L}(\beta\theta-\mu)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on $\Sigma$ if $L\in(0,\infty)$},

(3.85b)

\displaystyle m_{\Omega}(\phi)\nabla\mu\cdot\mathbf{n}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on $\Sigma$ if $L=\infty$}.

(3.85c)

Similarly, to deal with the first and the second line of (
3.84
), we assume
\phi
and
\psi
to fulfill one of the boundary conditions

\displaystyle\alpha\psi-\phi

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on $\Sigma$ if $K=0$},

(3.86a)

\displaystyle\varepsilon\nabla\phi\cdot\mathbf{n}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{K}(\alpha\psi-\phi)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on $\Sigma$ if $K\in(0,\infty)$},

(3.86b)

\displaystyle\nabla\phi\cdot\mathbf{n}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on $\Sigma$ if $K=\infty$},

(3.86c)

where
K\in[0,\infty]
is the number that was introduced in (
3.45
).
To show that (
3.84
) is actually fulfilled, we need to distinguish the cases
K=0
,
K\in(0,\infty)
and
K=\infty
.

•

The case

K=0
. Without loss of generality, we assume that
\alpha\neq 0
. The case
\alpha=0
can be handled similarly, but the computations are even easier.
Then, we immediately see that the second line in (
3.84
) vanishes. In the definition of
g
, we have
\chi(K)=\chi(0)=0
and thus
\partial_{\phi}g=0
on
\Sigma
. Moreover, due to (
3.86a
),
\alpha^{-1}\phi
can be interpreted as an extension of
\psi
in some neighborhood of
\Sigma
, which entails that
\partial_{t}^{\circ}\phi=\alpha\partial_{t}^{\circ}\psi
on
\Sigma
. Consequently, the first line of the right-hand side of (
3.84
) can be reformulated as

\displaystyle[\partial_{\psi}g-\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(\partial_{\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi}g)+\alpha\varepsilon\nabla\phi\cdot\mathbf{n}-\theta]\partial_{t}^{\circ}\psi\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

Choosing

\displaystyle\theta=\partial_{\psi}g-\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(\partial_{\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi}g)+\alpha\varepsilon\nabla\phi\cdot\mathbf{n}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma,

we thus ensure that the first line of (
3.84
) also vanishes. This shows that inequality (
3.84
) is fulfilled.

•

The case

K\in(0,\infty)
. Boundary condition (
3.86b
) entails that the second line of (
3.84
) vanishes.
In view of the definition of
g
, we further have

\displaystyle\partial_{\phi}g

\displaystyle=-\chi(K)(\alpha\psi-\phi)=-\varepsilon\nabla\phi\cdot\mathbf{n}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma,

\displaystyle\partial_{\psi}g

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\delta}G^{\prime}(\psi)+\alpha\chi(K)(\alpha\psi-\phi)=\frac{1}{\delta}G^{\prime}(\psi)+\alpha\varepsilon\nabla\phi\cdot\mathbf{n}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

This implies the first line of (
3.84
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle[\partial_{\psi}g-\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(\partial_{\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi}g)-\theta]\partial_{t}^{\circ}\psi\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

Hence, the choice

\displaystyle\theta=\partial_{\psi}g-\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(\partial_{\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi}g)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma,

ensures that the first line also vanishes. Consequently, inequality (
3.84
) is fulfilled.

•

The case

K=\infty
.
Since
\chi(K)=\chi(\infty)=0
, it directly follows from (
3.86c
) that the second line in (
3.84
) vanishes.
Moreover, since
\partial_{\phi}g=0
on
\Sigma
, the first line of (
3.84
) can be reformulated as

\displaystyle[\partial_{\psi}g-\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(\partial_{\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi}g)-\theta]\partial_{t}^{\circ}\psi\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

Thus, choosing

\displaystyle\theta=\partial_{\psi}g-\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(\partial_{\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi}g)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma,

ensures that the first line also vanishes.
This shows that inequality (
3.84
) is fulfilled.

By the definition of
g
(see (
3.45
)), we conclude in all cases
K\in[0,\infty]
that

\displaystyle\theta=-\delta\kappa\Delta_{\Gamma}\psi+\frac{1}{\delta}G^{\prime}(\psi)+\alpha\varepsilon\nabla\phi\cdot\mathbf{n}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

(3.87)

Eventually, substituting (
3.73
) and (
3.76
) into (
3.34
), we obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\psi+\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(\psi\mathbf{w})=\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(m_{\Gamma}(\psi)\nabla_{\Gamma}\theta)-\beta m_{\Omega}(\phi)\nabla\mu\cdot\mathbf{n}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma.

(3.88)

In summary, we have thus derived system (
1.9
) along with formulas (
1.2
) and (
1.10
)-(
1.13
) with the following correspondences:

(
1.9a
)

\widehat{=}

(
3.68
), (
3.27
),

(
1.9b
)

\widehat{=}

(
3.82
), (
3.28
),

(

)

\widehat{=}

(
3.12
),

(

)

\widehat{=}

(

),

(

)

\widehat{=}

(

),

(

)

\widehat{=}

(
3.88
),

(
1.9g
)

\widehat{=}

(

),

(

)

\widehat{=}

(

), (

),

(

)

(

),

4  Main results of the mathematical analysis

4.1  Assumptions

Before stating the main results, we first fix some assumptions that are supposed to hold throughout the remainder of this paper.

(A1)

We consider a bounded domain
\emptyset\neq\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
d\in\{2,3\}
with
C^{2}
-boundary
\Gamma\coloneqq\partial\Omega
and a final time
T>0
. We further use the notation

\displaystyle Q\coloneqq\Omega\times(0,T),\hskip 10.00002pt\Sigma\coloneqq\Gamma\times(0,T).

(A2)

The constants occurring in the system (
1.9
) satisfy
K,L\in[0,\infty]
,
\varepsilon,\delta,\kappa>0
,
\alpha\in[-1,1]
and
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
with
\alpha\beta\left|\Omega\right|+\left|\Gamma\right|\neq 0
. Since the choice of
\varepsilon,\delta
and
\kappa
has no impact on the mathematical analysis, we will simply set
\varepsilon=\delta=\kappa=1
without loss of generality.

(A3)

The density functions
\rho
and
\sigma
are given by

\displaystyle\rho(s)

\displaystyle=\frac{\tilde{\rho}_{2}-\tilde{\rho}_{1}}{2}s+\frac{\tilde{\rho}_{1}+\tilde{\rho}_{2}}{2},

\displaystyle\sigma(s)

\displaystyle=\frac{\tilde{\sigma}_{2}-\tilde{\sigma}_{1}}{2}s+\frac{\tilde{\sigma}_{1}+\tilde{\sigma}_{2}}{2}

for all
s\in[-1,1]
, respectively. Here,
\tilde{\rho}_{1},\tilde{\rho}_{2}>0
and
\tilde{\sigma}_{1},\tilde{\sigma}_{2}>0
are the specific densities of the two fluid components in the bulk and on the surface, respectively.
As the derivatives of
\rho
and
\sigma
are constant, we will usually write

\displaystyle\rho^{\prime}\equiv\rho^{\prime}(s)=\frac{\tilde{\rho}_{2}-\tilde{\rho}_{1}}{2}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\sigma^{\prime}\equiv\sigma^{\prime}(s)=\frac{\tilde{\sigma}_{2}-\tilde{\sigma}_{1}}{2}\,.

This means that
\rho^{\prime}
and
\sigma^{\prime}
are interpreted as constants. Moreover, we use the notation

\displaystyle\rho_{\ast}\coloneqq\min\{\tilde{\rho}_{1},\tilde{\rho}_{2}\},\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\rho^{\ast}\coloneqq\max\{\tilde{\rho}_{1},\tilde{\rho}_{2}\},

\displaystyle\sigma_{\ast}\coloneqq\min\{\tilde{\sigma}_{1},\tilde{\sigma}_{2}\},\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\sigma^{\ast}\coloneqq\max\{\tilde{\sigma}_{1},\tilde{\sigma}_{2}\},

By this definition, we clearly have

\displaystyle 0<\rho_{\ast}\leq\rho(s)\leq\rho^{\ast}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt0<\sigma_{\ast}\leq\sigma(s)\leq\sigma^{\ast}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all\penalty\ }s\in[-1,1].

(A4)

The mobility functions
m_{\Omega}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
m_{\Gamma}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
are assumed to be constant. Thus, without loss of generality, we assume that
m_{\Omega}=m_{\Gamma}=1
. Furthermore, the coefficients
\nu_{\Omega},\nu_{\Gamma}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
\gamma:\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
are assumed to be continuous, bounded, and uniformly positive. This means that there exist positive constants
\nu_{\ast},\nu^{\ast},\gamma_{\ast},\gamma^{\ast}
such that for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle 0<\nu_{\ast}\leq\nu_{\Omega}(s),\nu_{\Gamma}(s)\leq\nu^{\ast},

and, for all
(s,r)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

\displaystyle 0<\gamma_{\ast}\leq\gamma(s,r)\leq\gamma^{\ast}.

(A5)

We assume that the potentials
F,G:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
are of the form

\displaystyle F(s)=F_{1}(s)+F_{2}(s),\hskip 20.00003ptG(s)=G_{1}(s)+G_{2}(s)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for\penalty\ }s\in\mathbb{R},

where
F_{1},G_{1}\in C([-1,1])\cap C^{2}(-1,1)
,
F_{1}(s)=G_{1}(s)=+\infty
for all
s\in\mathbb{R}\setminus[-1,1]
,

\displaystyle\lim_{s\searrow-1}F_{1}^{\prime}(s)=\lim_{s\searrow-1}G_{1}^{\prime}(s)=-\infty\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\lim_{s\nearrow 1}F_{1}^{\prime}(s)=\lim_{s\nearrow 1}G_{1}^{\prime}(s)=+\infty,

and there exists a constant
\Theta>0
such that

\displaystyle F_{1}^{\prime\prime}(s)\geq\Theta\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptG_{1}^{\prime\prime}(s)\geq\Theta\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all\penalty\ }s\in(-1,1).

(4.1)

Without loss of generality, we assume that
F_{1}(0)=G_{1}(0)=0
and
F_{1}^{\prime}(0)=G_{1}^{\prime}(0)=0
. For
F_{2}
and
G_{2}
, we assume that
F_{2},G_{2}\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R})
with globally Lipschitz derivatives, respectively. Lastly, we require the singular part of the boundary potential to dominate the singular part of the bulk potential in the sense that there exist constants
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}>0
such that

\displaystyle\left|F_{1}^{\prime}(\alpha s)\right|\leq\kappa_{1}\left|G_{1}^{\prime}(s)\right|+\kappa_{2}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all\penalty\ }s\in(-1,1).

(4.2)

4.2  Main results

As pointed out in
(A2)
, we set
\varepsilon=\kappa=\delta=1
, as their particular choice has no impact on the mathematical analysis. Furthermore, as stated in
(A4)
, we will only consider the case of constant mobility functions
m_{\Omega}
and
m_{\Gamma}
, and therefore, we assume without loss of generality that
m_{\Omega}=m_{\Gamma}=1
. With these choices, and under consideration of (
1.14
), our system (
1.9
) reduces to

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\rho(\phi)\mathbf{v})+\textnormal{div}(\mathbf{v}\otimes(\rho(\phi)\mathbf{v}+\mathbf{J}))-\textnormal{div}(2\nu_{\Omega}(\phi)\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v})+\nabla\overline{p}=\mu\nabla\phi

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q

(4.3a)

\displaystyle\textnormal{div}\;\mathbf{v}=0

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q

(4.3b)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\sigma(\psi)\mathbf{w})+{\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}}(\mathbf{w}\otimes(\sigma(\psi)\mathbf{w}+\mathbf{K}))-{\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}}(2\nu_{\Gamma}(\psi)\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w})+\nabla_{\Gamma}\overline{q}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt=\theta\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi-2\nu_{\Omega}(\phi)\big[\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}\,\mathbf{n}\big]_{\tau}+\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathbf{J}\cdot\mathbf{n})\mathbf{w}+\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathbf{J}_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbf{n})\mathbf{w}-\gamma(\phi,\psi)\mathbf{w}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma,

(4.3c)

\displaystyle\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\;\mathbf{w}=0

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma

(4.3d)

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}|_{\Gamma}=\mathbf{w},\hskip 20.00003pt\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{n}=0

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma,

(4.3e)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\phi+\textnormal{div}(\phi\mathbf{v})=\Delta\mu

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q,

(4.3f)

\displaystyle\mu=-\Delta\phi+F^{\prime}(\phi)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in\penalty\ }Q,

(4.3g)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\psi+\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}(\psi\mathbf{w})=\Delta_{\Gamma}\theta-\beta\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\mu

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma,

(4.3h)

\displaystyle\theta=-\Delta_{\Gamma}\psi+G^{\prime}(\psi)+\alpha\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\phi

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma,

(4.3i)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}K\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\phi=\alpha\psi-\phi,&\text{if\penalty\ }K\in[0,\infty),\\
\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\phi=0,&\text{if\penalty\ }K=\infty,\end{cases}\hskip 10.00002pt\begin{cases}L\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\mu=\beta\theta-\mu,&\text{if\penalty\ }L\in[0,\infty),\\
\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\mu=0,&\text{if\penalty\ }L=\infty,\end{cases}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Sigma,

(4.3j)

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}|_{t=0}=\mathbf{v}_{0},\hskip 20.00003pt\phi|_{t=0}=\phi_{0}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in\penalty\ }\Omega,

(4.3k)

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}|_{t=0}=\mathbf{w}_{0},\hskip 20.00003pt\psi|_{t=0}=\psi_{0}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Gamma.

(4.3l)

The total energy associated with this system reads as

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{tot}}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w},\phi,\psi)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{2}\rho(\phi)\left|\mathbf{v}\right|^{2}\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}\frac{1}{2}\sigma(\psi)\left|\mathbf{w}\right|^{2}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma+\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{2}\left|\nabla\phi\right|^{2}+F(\phi)\;\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+\int_{\Gamma}\frac{1}{2}\left|\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\right|^{2}+G(\psi)\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma+\chi(K)\int_{\Gamma}\frac{1}{2}(\alpha\psi-\phi)^{2}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma.

The notion of weak solution to (
4.3
) is defined as follows:

Definition 4.1
.

Suppose the assumptions
(A1)
-
(A5)
hold. Let
T>0
,
K,L\in[0,\infty]
,
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})\in\mathbfcal{L}^{2}_{\textnormal{div}}
, and
(\phi_{0},\psi_{0})\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{K,\alpha}
such that

\displaystyle\|\phi_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq 1,\hskip 20.00003pt\|\psi_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma)}\leq 1.

(4.4a)

Furthermore, we assume that

\displaystyle\beta\textnormal{mean}\left(\phi_{0},\psi_{0}\right)\in(-1,1),\hskip 20.00003pt\textnormal{mean}\left(\phi_{0},\psi_{0}\right)\in(-1,1),\hskip 20.00003pt\text{if\penalty\ }L\in[0,\infty),

(4.4b)

and

\displaystyle{\langle\phi_{0}\rangle}_{\Omega}\in(-1,1),\hskip 20.00003pt{\langle\psi_{0}\rangle}_{\Gamma}\in(-1,1),\hskip 20.00003pt\text{if\penalty\ }L=\infty.

(4.4c)

The sextuplet
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w},\phi,\psi,\mu,\theta)
is called a weak solution of the system (
4.3
) on
[0,T]
if the following properties hold:

(i)

The functions
\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w},\phi,\psi,\mu
and
\theta
have the regularities

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w}\right)

\displaystyle\in C_{w}([0,T];\mathbfcal{L}^{2}_{\textnormal{div}})\cap L^{2}(0,T;\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0,\textnormal{div}}),

(4.5a)

\displaystyle\left(\phi,\psi\right)

\displaystyle\in C([0,T];\mathcal{L}^{2})\cap H^{1}(0,T;(\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta})^{\prime})\cap L^{\infty}(0,T;\mathcal{H}^{1}_{K,\alpha}),

(4.5b)

\displaystyle\left(\mu,\theta\right)

\displaystyle\in L^{2}(0,T;\mathcal{H}_{L,\beta}^{1}),

(4.5c)

\displaystyle\left(F^{\prime}(\phi),G^{\prime}(\psi)\right)

\displaystyle\in L^{2}(0,T;\mathcal{L}^{2}),

(4.5d)

and it holds
\left|\phi\right|<1
a.e. in
Q
and
\left|\psi\right|<1
a.e. on
\Sigma
.

(ii)

The initial conditions are satisfied in the following sense:

\displaystyle(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})|_{t=0}=(\mathbf{v}_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})\hskip 10.00002pt\text{a.e.\penalty\ in\penalty\ }\Omega\times\Gamma,\hskip 20.00003pt(\phi,\psi)|_{t=0}=(\phi_{0},\psi_{0})\hskip 10.00002pt\text{a.e.\penalty\ in\penalty\ }\Omega\times\Gamma.

(4.6)

(iii)

The variational formulations

\displaystyle-\big(\rho(\phi)\mathbf{v},\partial_{t}\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\big)_{L^{2}(Q)}-\big(\sigma(\psi)\mathbf{w},\partial_{t}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\big)_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}+\big((\mathbf{J}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{v},\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\big)_{L^{2}(Q)}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\big((\mathbf{K}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma})\mathbf{w},\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\big)_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}+\big(\rho(\phi)(\mathbf{v}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{v},\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\big)_{L^{2}(Q)}+\big(\sigma(\psi)(\mathbf{w}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma})\mathbf{w},\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\big)_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\big(2\nu_{\Omega}(\phi)\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v},\mathbf{D}\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\big)_{L^{2}(Q)}+\big(2\nu_{\Gamma}(\psi)\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w},\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\big)_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}+\big(\gamma(\phi,\psi)\mathbf{w},\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\big)_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}

(4.7a)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt=\big(\mu\nabla\phi,\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\big)_{L^{2}(Q)}+\big(\theta\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi,\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\big)_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}+\tfrac{1}{2}\chi(L)(\beta\sigma^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime})\big((\beta\theta-\mu)\mathbf{w},\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\big)_{L^{2}(\Sigma)},

\displaystyle\big(\phi,\partial_{t}\zeta\big)_{L^{2}(Q)}+\big(\psi,\partial_{t}\xi\big)_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}+\big(\phi\mathbf{v},\nabla\zeta\big)_{L^{2}(Q)}+\big(\psi\mathbf{w},\nabla_{\Gamma}\xi\big)_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}

(4.7b)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt=\big(\nabla\mu,\nabla\zeta\big)_{L^{2}(Q)}+\big(\nabla_{\Gamma}\theta,\nabla_{\Gamma}\xi\big)_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}+\chi(L)\big(\beta\theta-\mu,\beta\xi-\zeta\big)_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}

\displaystyle\big(\mu,\eta\big)_{L^{2}(Q)}+\big(\theta,\vartheta\big)_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt=\big(\nabla\phi,\nabla\eta\big)_{L^{2}(Q)}+\big(F^{\prime}(\phi),\eta\big)_{L^{2}(Q)}+\big(\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi,\nabla_{\Gamma}\vartheta\big)_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}+\big(G^{\prime}(\psi),\vartheta\big)_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}

(4.7c)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\chi(K)\big(\alpha\psi-\phi,\alpha\vartheta-\eta\big)_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}

where
\mathbf{J}=-\rho^{\prime}\,\nabla\mu
and
\mathbf{K}=-\sigma^{\prime}\,\nabla_{\Gamma}\theta
,
hold for all test functions
(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}})\in C_{c}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbfcal{H}_{0,\textnormal{div}}^{2})
,
(\zeta,\xi)\in C_{c}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathcal{H}^{1}_{L,\beta})
and
(\eta,\vartheta)\in L^{2}(0,T;\mathcal{H}^{1}_{K,\alpha})
.

(iv)

The functions
\phi
and
\psi
satisfy the mass conservation law

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\beta\int_{\Omega}\phi(t)\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}\psi(t)\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma=\beta\int_{\Omega}\phi_{0}\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}\psi_{0}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma,&\textnormal{if\penalty\ }L\in[0,\infty),\\
\int_{\Omega}\phi(t)\;\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\Omega}\phi_{0}\;\mathrm{d}x\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\int_{\Gamma}\psi(t)\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma=\int_{\Gamma}\psi_{0}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma,&\textnormal{if\penalty\ }L=\infty,\end{cases}

(4.8)

for all
t\in[0,T]
.

(v)

The energy inequality

\displaystyle E(\mathbf{v}(t),\mathbf{w}(t),\phi(t),\psi(t))+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}2\nu_{\Omega}(\phi)\left|\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}\right|^{2}\;\mathrm{d}x\;\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Gamma}2\nu_{\Gamma}(\psi)\left|\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}\right|^{2}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma\;\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Gamma}\gamma(\phi,\psi)\left|\mathbf{w}\right|^{2}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma\;\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\left|\nabla\mu\right|^{2}\;\mathrm{d}x\;\mathrm{d}s

(4.9)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Gamma}\left|\nabla_{\Gamma}\theta\right|^{2}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma\;\mathrm{d}s+\chi(L)\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Gamma}(\beta\theta-\mu)^{2}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma\;\mathrm{d}s\leq E(\mathbf{v}_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0},\phi_{0},\psi_{0})

holds for all
t\in[0,T)
.

We are now in a position to state the main theorem of this paper.

Theorem 4.2
.

Suppose the assumptions
(A1)
-
(A5)
hold. Let
K,L\in[0,\infty]
,
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})\in\mathbfcal{L}^{2}_{\textnormal{div}}
and let
(\phi_{0},\psi_{0})\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{K,\alpha}
satisfy (
4.4
). If
L=0
, additionally assume that

\displaystyle\beta(\tilde{\sigma}_{2}-\tilde{\sigma}_{1})=-(\tilde{\rho}_{2}-\tilde{\rho}_{1}).

(4.10)

Then, there exists at least one weak solution
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w},\phi,\psi,\mu,\theta)
to system (
4.3
) in the sense of Definition
4.1
.

The theorem is proved in two steps.

Step 1:
For
L\in(0,\infty]
, we prove the existence of a weak solution via a semi-Galerkin scheme combined with a fixed-point argument. In this approach, the Galerkin basis to discretize the bulk-surface Navier–Stokes subsystem (
4.3a
)-(
4.3e
) consists of eigenfunctions of a related
bulk-surface Stokes system
(cf. (
5.1
)). Therefore, the proof crucially relies on the well-posedness results, the regularity theory, and the spectral theory for the bulk-surface Stokes problem, which are developed in Section
5
.
The statement of Theorem
4.2
in the case
L\in(0,\infty]
is then shown in Theorem
6.1
.

Step 2:
For
L=0
, the procedure described in Step 1 does not work since admissible test functions in (
4.7b
) need to satisfy the corresponding trace relation. Instead, we construct a weak solution via the asymptotic limit
L\to 0
of weak solutions associated with
L>0
. This result is shown in Theorem
7.1
.

This means that Theorem
4.2
is established by combining Theorem
6.1
and Theorem
7.1
.
∎

Remark 4.3
.

We point out that, since
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\in L^{2}(0,T;\mathbfcal{L}^{2}_{\textnormal{div}})
, any weak solution
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w},\phi,\psi,\mu,\theta)
automatically satisfies

\displaystyle(\phi,\psi)\in L^{2}(0,T;\mathcal{W}^{2,6}),\hskip 20.00003pt(F^{\prime}(\phi),G^{\prime}(\psi))\in L^{2}(0,T;\mathcal{L}^{6}),

according to
[
39
, Theorem 3.2]
.
Consequently, the equations

\displaystyle\mu

\displaystyle=-\Delta\phi+F^{\prime}(\phi)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{a.e.\penalty\ in\penalty\ }Q,

(4.11a)

\displaystyle\theta

\displaystyle=-\Delta_{\Gamma}\psi+G^{\prime}(\psi)+\alpha\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\phi,

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{a.e.\penalty\ on\penalty\ }\Sigma,

(4.11b)

\displaystyle K\partial_{\mathbf{n}}\phi

\displaystyle=\alpha\psi-\phi

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{a.e.\penalty\ on\penalty\ }\Sigma

(4.11c)

are fulfilled in the strong sense.
Moreover, if
K\in(0,\infty]
, we have

\displaystyle(\phi,\psi)\in L^{4}(0,T;\mathcal{H}^{2}),

whereas in the case
K=0
, we only have

\displaystyle(\phi,\psi)\in L^{3}(0,T;\mathcal{H}^{2}),

see
[
39
, Theorem 3.3]
.

Remark 4.4
.

The compatibility condition (
4.10
) in the case
L=0
means that
\beta\sigma^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime}=0
. Therefore, it ensures that the term

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\chi(L)(\beta\sigma^{\prime}+\rho^{\prime})\big((\beta\theta-\mu)\mathbf{w},\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\big)_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}

in the weak formulation (
4.7
) vanishes, which is crucial in the proof of Theorem
7.1
. Condition (
4.10
) is related to the compatibility condition
\tilde{\rho}_{1}=\tilde{\rho}_{2}
that was imposed in
[
32
]
for the analysis of system (
1.1
) from
[
38
]
with
L=0
. From the viewpoint of mathematical analysis, this is an advantage of the new model (
1.9
) compared to (
1.1
) since even in the case
L=0
we can allow for unmatched densities of the fluids.

Under additional assumptions on the initial data
(\phi_{0},\psi_{0})
, we immediately infer that the solution component
(\phi,\psi,\mu,\theta)
satisfies the Cahn–Hilliard subsystem (
4.3f
)-(
4.3j
) in the strong sense.

Theorem 4.5
.

Suppose the assumptions
(A1)
-
(A5)
hold. Let
K\in[0,\infty]
,
L\in(0,\infty]
,
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})\in\mathbfcal{L}^{2}_{\textnormal{div}}
and let
(\phi_{0},\psi_{0})\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{K,\alpha}
satisfy (
4.4
). We further assume that the following compatibility condition holds:

(C)

There exists
\left(\mu_{0},\theta_{0}\right)\in\mathcal{H}^{1}
such that for all
\left(\eta,\vartheta\right)\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{K,\alpha}
, it holds

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\int_{\Omega}\mu_{0}\eta\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}\theta_{0}\eta_{\Gamma}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma\\
&=\int_{\Omega}\nabla\phi_{0}\cdot\nabla\eta+F^{\prime}(\phi_{0})\eta\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi_{0}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\vartheta+G^{\prime}(\psi_{0})\vartheta\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma\\
&\quad+\chi(K)\int_{\Gamma}(\alpha\psi_{0}-\phi_{0})(\alpha\vartheta-\eta)\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma.\end{aligned}

Let
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w},\phi,\psi,\mu,\theta)
be a corresponding weak solution in the sense of Definition
4.1
. Then
(\phi,\psi,\mu,\theta)
has the following regularity:

\displaystyle(\phi,\psi)

\displaystyle\in L^{\infty}(0,T;\mathcal{W}^{2,6})\cap\big(C(\overline{Q})\times C(\overline{\Sigma})\big),

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}\phi,\partial_{t}\psi)

\displaystyle\in L^{\infty}(0,T;(\mathcal{H}^{1})^{\prime})\cap L^{2}(0,T;\mathcal{H}^{1}),

\displaystyle(\mu,\theta)

\displaystyle\in L^{\infty}(0,T;\mathcal{H}^{1})\cap L^{2}(0,T;\mathcal{H}^{2}),

\displaystyle(F^{\prime}(\phi),G^{\prime}(\psi))

\displaystyle\in L^{2}(0,T;\mathcal{L}^{\infty})\cap L^{\infty}(0,T;\mathcal{L}^{6}).

The assertion follows immediately from
[
39
, Theorem 4.1]
since
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\in L^{\infty}(0,T;\mathcal{L}^{2})\cap L^{2}(0,T;\mathcal{H}^{1}_{0,\textnormal{div}})
.
∎

Remark 4.6
.

If
d=2
, the statement of Theorem
4.5
also holds in the case
L=0
, see
[
39
, Remark 3.6]
. However, it is not yet clear whether the statement remains true in the case
L=0
if
d=3
.

5  Analysis of a bulk-surface Stokes system

In this section, we analyze a new bulk-surface Stokes system and prove the existence of a unique strong solution. Afterwards, we study the corresponding bulk-surface Stokes operator and its spectral properties. These results are essential for our proof of Theorem
6.1
as we use the eigenfunctions of the bulk-surface Stokes operator as the basis functions in our semi-Galerkin scheme.

To this end, we consider the system

\displaystyle-\textnormal{div}(2\nu_{\Omega}(\phi)\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v})+\nabla p

\displaystyle=\mathbf{f}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in\penalty\ }\Omega,

(5.1a)

\displaystyle\textnormal{div}\,\mathbf{v}

\displaystyle=g

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in\penalty\ }\Omega,

(5.1b)

\displaystyle-{\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}}(2\nu_{\Gamma}(\psi)\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w})+2\nu_{\Omega}(\phi)\big[\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}\,\mathbf{n}\big]_{\tau}+\nabla_{\Gamma}q+\gamma(\phi,\psi)\mathbf{w}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{f}_{\Gamma}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Gamma,

(5.1c)

\displaystyle\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\,\mathbf{w}

\displaystyle=g_{\Gamma}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Gamma,

(5.1d)

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}|_{\Gamma}=\mathbf{w},\hskip 10.00002pt\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{n}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Gamma,

(5.1e)

for given source terms
(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{f}_{\Gamma})\in(\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0})^{\prime}
,
(g,g_{\Gamma})\in\mathcal{L}^{2}_{(0)}
and measurable functions
\phi:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
\psi:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. Furthermore, the viscosity coefficients
\nu_{\Omega},\nu_{\Gamma}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and the friction coefficient
\gamma:\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfy the assumptions stated in
(A4)
.

5.1  Existence and uniqueness of weak solutions

We start our analysis by considering the case of incompressible fluids, i.e.,
g=0
in
\Omega
and
g_{\Gamma}=0
on
\Gamma
. In this case, the weak formulation of (
5.1
) reads as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\int_{\Omega}2\nu_{\Omega}(\phi)\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}:\mathbf{D}\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}2\nu_{\Gamma}(\psi)\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}:\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma+\int_{\Gamma}\gamma(\phi,\psi)\mathbf{w}\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma\\
&\qquad=\big<(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{f}_{\Gamma}),(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}})\big>_{\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0}}\end{split}

(5.2)

for all
(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}})\in\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0,\textnormal{div}}
.

To prove the existence of a unique weak solution to (
5.1
), we start by establishing the following bulk-surface Korn-type inequality, which yields the coercivity of the bilinear form corresponding to the problem.

Lemma 5.1
.

There exists a constant
C_{K}>0
such that

\displaystyle\|(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\|_{\mathbfcal{H}^{1}}\leq C_{K}\big(\|\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\|\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}+\|\mathbf{w}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}\big)

(5.3)

for all
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\in\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0}
.

First, we recall the surface Korn inequality: There exists a constant
C_{\Gamma}>0
such that

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Gamma)}\leq C_{\Gamma}\big(\|\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}+\|\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}\big)

(5.4)

for all
\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}\in\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Gamma)
, see, e.g.
[
47
, Formula (4.7)]
. Furthermore, the standard Korn inequality (see
[
16
, Remark IV.7.3.]
) states that there exists a constant
C_{\Omega}>0
such that

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Omega)}\leq C_{\Omega}\|\mathbf{D}\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)}

(5.5)

for all
\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}\in\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Omega)
with
\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}|_{\Gamma}=\mathbf{0}
a.e. on
\Gamma
.

Let now
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\in\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0}
be arbitrary.
According to the inverse trace theorem (see
[
46
, Theorem 4.2.3]
), there exists a function
\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\in H^{3/2}(\Omega)
such that
\widehat{\mathbf{w}}=\mathbf{w}
a.e. on
\Gamma
and there is a constant
C_{\mathrm{tr}}>0
independent of
\widehat{\mathbf{w}}
and
\mathbf{w}
such that

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\|_{H^{3/2}(\Omega)}\leq C_{\mathrm{tr}}\,\|\mathbf{w}\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma)}.

(5.6)

In particular, we have
(\mathbf{v}-\widehat{\mathbf{w}})|_{\Gamma}=0
a.e. on
\Gamma
. Hence, using Korn’s inequality (
5.5
) and the inverse trace estimate (
5.6
), we get

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{v}-\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\Omega}\big(\|\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\|\mathbf{D}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\big)

\displaystyle\leq C_{\Omega}\big(\|\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+C_{\mathrm{tr}}\|\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Gamma)}\big).

Finally, using the surface Korn inequality (
5.4
), we conclude that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{v}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\|\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Omega)}+\|\mathbf{v}-\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\Omega}\|\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+(1+C_{\Omega}C_{\mathrm{tr}})\|\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Gamma)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{K}\big(\|\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\|\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}+\|\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)}\big)

for some constant
C_{K}>0
that is independent of
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})
. This proves the claim.
∎

Thus, considering the bilinear form

\displaystyle\mathbfcal{B}:\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0,\textnormal{div}}\times\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0,\textnormal{div}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle((\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w}),(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}}))

\displaystyle\mapsto\int_{\Omega}2\nu_{\Omega}(\phi)\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}:\mathbf{D}\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}2\nu_{\Gamma}(\psi)\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}:\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt+\int_{\Gamma}\gamma(\phi,\psi)\mathbf{w}\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma,

we infer from Lemma
5.1
and the assumptions on the coefficients (see
(A4)
) that
\mathbfcal{B}
is coercive. Consequently, employing the Lax–Milgram theorem, for every
(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{f}_{\Gamma})\in(\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0})^{\prime}
, there exists a unique pair
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\in\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0,\textnormal{div}}
, which fulfills (
5.2
) for all
(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}})\in\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0,\textnormal{div}}
. In particular, we have

\displaystyle\|(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\|_{\mathbfcal{H}^{1}}\leq\frac{C_{K}}{\min\{2\nu_{\ast},\gamma_{\ast}\}}\|(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{f}_{\Gamma})\|_{(\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0})^{\prime}}.

Next, to reconstruct the pressure pair
(p,q)
, we need the following lemma, which is an adaptation of
[
55
, Lemma 3.2]
.

Lemma 5.2
.

The operator

\displaystyle\underline{\textnormal{div}}:\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0}/\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0,\textnormal{div}}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}^{2}_{(0)},\hskip 10.00002pt(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\mapsto(\textnormal{div}\,\mathbf{v},\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\,\mathbf{w}),

(5.7)

is an isomorphism. In particular, there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\|(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\|_{\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0}/\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0,\textnormal{div}}}\leq C\|(\textnormal{div}\,\mathbf{v},\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\,\mathbf{w})\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}_{(0)}}.

(5.8)

We start by considering the operator

\displaystyle\widetilde{\underline{\textnormal{div}}}:\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}^{2}_{(0)},\hskip 10.00002pt(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\mapsto(\textnormal{div}\,\mathbf{v},\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\,\mathbf{w}).

This operator is clearly
well-defined with
\mathrm{ker}\,\widetilde{\underline{\textnormal{div}}}=\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0,\textnormal{div}}
. Now, we show that
\widetilde{\underline{\textnormal{div}}}
is surjective. To this end, let
(g,g_{\Gamma})\in\mathcal{L}^{2}_{(0)}
be arbitrary. Then, let
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Gamma)
with
\mathbf{w}\cdot\mathbf{n}=0
be a solution of

\displaystyle\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\,\mathbf{w}=g_{\Gamma}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Gamma.

The existence of such a solution
\mathbf{w}
can be shown by
employing
L^{2}
-theory for the Laplace–Beltrami operator on
\Gamma
, noticing that the right-hand side is mean-value free.
Moreover, utilizing
L^{2}
-theory of the divergence operator in
\Omega
, we find
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Omega)
such that

\displaystyle\textnormal{div}\,\mathbf{v}

\displaystyle=g

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\text{in\penalty\ }\Omega,

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{w}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Gamma.

We have therefore shown the existence of
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\in\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0}
satisfying

\displaystyle\widetilde{\underline{\textnormal{div}}}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})=(g,g_{\Gamma}),

which proves that
\widetilde{\underline{\textnormal{div}}}:\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}^{2}_{(0)}
is a surjective homomorphism. Consequently, by the fundamental theorem of homomorphisms,
\underline{\textnormal{div}}:\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0}/\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0,\textnormal{div}}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}^{2}_{(0)}
is an isomorphism.
∎

As a corollary, we obtain the following (see also
[
55
, Corollary 3.3]
).

Corollary 5.3
.

The operator
\underline{\nabla}:\mathcal{L}^{2}_{(0)}\mapsto(\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0,\textnormal{div}})^{\perp}\subset(\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0})^{\prime}
, defined by

\displaystyle\underline{\nabla}(p,q)(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})=-\int_{\Omega}p\,\textnormal{div}\,\mathbf{v}\;\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\Gamma}q\,\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\,\mathbf{w}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma,

(5.9)

for
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\in\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0}
, is an isomorphism. We have

\displaystyle\|(p,q)\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}_{(0)}}\leq C\|\underline{\nabla}(p,q)\|_{(\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0})^{\prime}}

(5.10)

with the same constant
C
as in Lemma
5.2
.

This follows from Lemma
5.2
, the fact that

\displaystyle\underline{\nabla}=-\underline{\textnormal{div}}^{\prime}:(\mathcal{L}^{2}_{(0)})^{\prime}\rightarrow(\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0}/\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0,\textnormal{div}})^{\prime}

is an isomorphism, from the isometry
(\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0}/\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0,\textnormal{div}})^{\prime}\simeq(\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0,\textnormal{div}})^{\perp}
, and by Riesz’ isometry
\mathcal{L}^{2}_{(0)}\simeq(\mathcal{L}^{2}_{(0)})^{\prime}
. Recall also that forming the adjoint preserves the operator norm.
∎

Now, Corollary
5.3
allows us to reconstruct the pressure pair
(p,q)
. Indeed, consider the unique weak solution
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})
that was constructed above by the Lax–Milgram theorem. Then, by Corollary
5.3
, we find a unique pair
(p,q)\in\mathcal{L}^{2}_{(0)}
such that

\displaystyle\underline{\nabla}(p,q)(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}})

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Omega}2\nu_{\Omega}(\phi)\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}:\mathbf{D}\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\;\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\Gamma}2\nu_{\Gamma}(\psi)\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}:\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma-\int_{\Gamma}\gamma(\phi,\psi)\mathbf{w}\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\big<(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{f}_{\Gamma}),(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}})\big>_{\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0}}

for all
(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}})\in\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0}
, from which we deduce the weak formulation

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\int_{\Omega}2\nu_{\Omega}(\phi)\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}:\mathbf{D}\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}2\nu_{\Gamma}(\psi)\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}:\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma+\int_{\Gamma}\gamma(\phi,\psi)\mathbf{w}\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma\\
&\qquad-\int_{\Omega}p\,\textnormal{div}\,\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\;\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\Gamma}q\,\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\,\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma\\
&\quad=\big<(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{f}_{\Gamma}),(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}})\big>_{\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0}}\end{split}

(5.11)

for all
(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}})\in\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0}
.

We summarize our considerations above in the following theorem.

Theorem 5.4
.

Let
(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{f}_{\Gamma})\in(\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0})^{\prime}
and
(g,g_{\Gamma})=(0,0)
. Then there exists a unique weak solution
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\in\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0}
of the bulk-surface stokes system (
5.1
) and an associated pressure pair
(p,q)\in\mathcal{L}^{2}_{(0)}
such that (
5.11
) is fulfilled for all
(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}})\in\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0}
. Additionally, there exists a constant
C>0
, that depends only on the data of the system, such that

\displaystyle\|(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\|_{\mathbfcal{H}^{1}}+\|(p,q)\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}}\leq C\|(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{f}_{\Gamma})\|_{(\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0})^{\prime}}.

(5.12)

Now, we want to study the system (
5.1
) with general divergences. To this end, we consider
(g,g_{\Gamma})\in\mathcal{L}^{2}_{(0)}
. Then, by Lemma
5.2
, we find a unique pair
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})\in\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0}
such that

\displaystyle\underline{\textnormal{div}}(\mathbf{v}_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})=(g,g_{\Gamma}).

(5.13)

Next, we define
(\tilde{\mathbf{f}},\tilde{\mathbf{f}}_{\Gamma})\in(\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0})^{\prime}
via

\displaystyle\begin{split}\big<(\tilde{\mathbf{f}},\tilde{\mathbf{f}}_{\Gamma}),(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}})\big>_{\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0}}&\coloneqq\big<(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{f}_{\Gamma}),(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}})\big>_{\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0}}-\int_{\Omega}2\nu_{\Omega}(\phi)\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}_{0}:\mathbf{D}\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\;\mathrm{d}x\\
&\qquad-\int_{\Gamma}2\nu_{\Gamma}(\psi)\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}_{0}:\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma-\int_{\Gamma}\gamma(\phi,\psi)\mathbf{w}_{0}\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma\end{split}

(5.14)

for
(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}})\in\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0}
. Employing again the bulk-surface Korn inequality from Lemma
5.1
, the Lax–Milgram theorem provides the existence of a unique pair
(\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{w}_{1})\in\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0,\textnormal{div}}
, which solves

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\int_{\Omega}2\nu_{\Omega}(\phi)\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}_{1}:\mathbf{D}\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}2\nu_{\Gamma}(\psi)\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}_{1}:\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma+\int_{\Gamma}\gamma(\phi,\psi)\mathbf{w}_{1}\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma\\
&\qquad=\big<(\tilde{\mathbf{f}},\tilde{\mathbf{f}}_{\Gamma}),(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}})\big>_{\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0}}\end{split}

(5.15)

for all
(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}})\in\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0,\textnormal{div}}
. Then, by Corollary
5.3
, there exists a unique pair
(p,q)\in\mathcal{L}^{2}_{(0)}
such that

\displaystyle\underline{\nabla}(p,q)(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}})

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Omega}2\nu_{\Omega}(\phi)\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}_{1}:\mathbf{D}\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\;\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\Gamma}2\nu_{\Gamma}(\psi)\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}_{1}:\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt-\int_{\Gamma}\gamma(\phi,\psi)\mathbf{w}_{1}\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma+\big<(\tilde{\mathbf{f}},\tilde{\mathbf{f}}_{\Gamma}),(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}})\big>_{\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0}}

for all
(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}})\in\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0}
. Now, we define
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\coloneqq(\mathbf{v}_{0}+\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{w}_{0}+\mathbf{w}_{1})
. Then

\displaystyle\textnormal{div}\,\mathbf{v}

\displaystyle=\textnormal{div}\,\mathbf{v}_{0}+\textnormal{div}\,\mathbf{v}_{1}=g

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\text{in\penalty\ }\Omega,

\displaystyle\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\,\mathbf{w}

\displaystyle=\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\,\mathbf{w}_{0}+\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\,\mathbf{w}_{1}=g_{\Gamma}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Gamma,

and, by construction of
(p,q)
and the definition of
(\tilde{\mathbf{f}},\tilde{\mathbf{f}}_{\Gamma})
, we readily notice that
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\in\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0}
solves

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\int_{\Omega}\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}:\mathbf{D}\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}:\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma+\int_{\Gamma}\gamma(\phi,\psi)\mathbf{w}\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma\\
&\qquad-\int_{\Omega}p\,\textnormal{div}\,\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\;\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\Gamma}q\,\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\,\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma\\
&\quad=\big<(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{f}_{\Gamma}),(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}})\big>_{\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0}}\end{split}

(5.16)

for all
(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}})\in\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0}
, together with

\displaystyle\textnormal{div}\,\mathbf{v}=g\ \text{in\penalty\ }\Omega,\hskip 20.00003pt\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\,\mathbf{w}=g_{\Gamma}\ \text{on\penalty\ }\Gamma.

This means that the following result is established.

Theorem 5.5
.

Let
(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{f}_{\Gamma})\in(\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0})^{\prime}
and
(g,g_{\Gamma})\in\mathcal{L}^{2}_{(0)}
. Then there exists a unique weak solution
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\in\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0}
of the bulk-surface stokes system (
5.1
) and an associated pressure pair
(p,q)\in\mathcal{L}^{2}_{(0)}
such that (
5.16
) is fulfilled for all
(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}})\in\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0}
.

5.2  Existence and uniqueness of strong solutions

In the next step, assuming that the coefficients
\nu_{\Omega},\nu_{\Gamma}
and
\gamma
are positive constants, we prove that the system (
5.1
) admits a unique strong solution. In the following, we discuss only the case of incompressible fluids, as it is the more relevant case for our subsequent analysis of system (
1.9
). The proof of the existence of a unique strong solution relies on a fixed-point argument. To this end, we start by considering the surface Stokes equation and the corresponding surface Stokes operator.

In this subsection, let
r\in[2,\infty)
be arbitrary. We consider the surface Helmholtz projection

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{\textnormal{div}}^{\Gamma}:\mathbf{L}^{r}_{\tau}(\Gamma)\to\mathbf{L}^{r}_{\textnormal{div}}(\Gamma),\hskip 10.00002pt\mathbf{P}_{\textnormal{div}}^{\Gamma}(\mathbf{w})=\mathbf{w}-\nabla_{\Gamma}q,

(5.17)

where
q\in\dot{W}^{1,r}(\Gamma)
is (up to an additive constant) uniquely determined by

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}\nabla_{\Gamma}q\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\xi\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma=\int_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\xi\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all\penalty\ }\xi\in\dot{W}^{1,r^{\prime}}(\Gamma),

(5.18)

cf.
[
73
, Lemma A.1]
.
Here,
r^{\prime}
denotes the dual Sobolev exponent of
r
, i.e.,
r^{\prime}=\frac{r}{r-1}
.
The existence of this projection is also related to the surface Helmholtz decomposition, which can be found, for example, in
[
67
, Theorem 4.2]
.
According to
[
73
, Sect. 2]
, we further have

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}\mathbf{P}_{\textnormal{div}}^{\Gamma}(\mathbf{w})\cdot\mathbf{u}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma=\int_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}\cdot\mathbf{P}_{\textnormal{div}}^{\Gamma}(\mathbf{u})\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma

(5.19)

for all
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbf{L}^{r}_{\tau}(\Gamma)
and
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{L}^{r^{\prime}}_{\tau}(\Gamma)
. Therefore, we define the surface Stokes operator by

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}^{\Gamma}(\mathbf{w})=-\mathbf{P}_{\textnormal{div}}^{\Gamma}{\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}}(2\nu_{\Gamma}\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w})\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all\penalty\ }\mathbf{w}\in D(\mathbf{A}^{\Gamma})=\mathbf{W}^{2,r}_{\tau}(\Gamma)\cap\mathbf{L}^{r}_{\textnormal{div}}(\Gamma).

(5.20)

We further refer to
[
65
,
73
]
for the surface Stokes operator on more general surfaces, to
[
7
]
for the surface Stokes operator on evolving surfaces, and to the survey article
[
44
]
for the Stokes operator in various other geometric settings.

The following theorem ensures the existence of a unique strong solution to the surface Stokes problem.

Theorem 5.6
.

Let
\mathbf{f}_{\Gamma}\in\mathbf{L}^{r}_{\tau}(\Gamma)
. Then there exists a unique strong solution
(\mathbf{w},q)
with
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbf{W}^{2,r}_{\tau}(\Gamma)\cap\mathbf{L}^{r}_{\textnormal{div}}(\Gamma)
and
q\in W^{1,r}_{(0)}(\Gamma)
of the surface Stokes problem

\displaystyle-{\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}}(2\nu_{\Gamma}\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w})+\nabla_{\Gamma}q+\gamma\mathbf{w}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{f}_{\Gamma}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Gamma,

(5.21)

\displaystyle\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\,\mathbf{w}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Gamma.

(5.22)

Moreover, there exists a constant
C>0
, independent of
\mathbf{f}_{\Gamma}
, such that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{w}\|_{\mathbf{W}^{2,r}(\Gamma)}+\|q\|_{W^{1,r}(\Gamma)}\leq C\|\mathbf{f}_{\Gamma}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{r}(\Gamma)}.

(5.23)

The definition of the projection
\mathbf{P}_{\textnormal{div}}^{\Gamma}
implies that there exists a function
q_{1}\in\dot{W}^{1,r}(\Gamma)
such that

\displaystyle\mathbf{f}_{\Gamma}=\mathbf{P}_{\textnormal{div}}^{\Gamma}\mathbf{f}_{\Gamma}+\nabla_{\Gamma}q_{1}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{a.e.\penalty\ on $\Gamma$.}

(5.24)

We further know from
[
65
, Corollary 3.4]
that the operator
\mathbf{A}^{\Gamma}
has maximal
L^{p}
-regularity. Hence,
\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\mathrm{Re}\,z<0\}\subset\rho(\mathbf{A}^{\Gamma})
,
and there exists a constant
M\geq 1
such that for all
z\in\mathbb{C}
with
\mathrm{Re}\,z>0
,
z+\mathbf{A}^{\Gamma}
is invertible with

\displaystyle\|(z+\mathbf{A}^{\Gamma})^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\frac{M}{1+\left|z\right|},

(5.25)

see, e.g.,
[
64
, Proposition 3.5.2]
. Consequently, we find
\mathbf{w}\in D(\mathbf{A}^{\Gamma})
such that

\displaystyle\gamma\mathbf{w}+\mathbf{A}^{\Gamma}(\mathbf{w})=\mathbf{P}_{\textnormal{div}}^{\Gamma}\mathbf{f}_{\Gamma}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{a.e.\penalty\ on $\Gamma$}.

(5.26)

In view of (
5.25
), it holds that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{w}\|_{\mathbf{W}^{2,r}(\Gamma)}\leq\frac{M}{1+\gamma}\|\mathbf{f}_{\Gamma}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{r}(\Gamma)}.

As
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbf{W}^{2,r}_{\tau}(\Gamma)\cap\mathbf{L}^{r}_{\textnormal{div}}(\Gamma)
, we further have

\displaystyle-{\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}}(2\nu_{\Gamma}\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}),\;\mathbf{A}^{\Gamma}(\mathbf{w})\in\mathbf{L}^{r}(\Gamma)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003pt\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\,\mathbf{w}=0\hskip 10.00002pt\text{a.e.\penalty\ on $\Gamma$}.

Hence, by the definition of the projection
\mathbf{P}_{\textnormal{div}}^{\Gamma}
, we find a function
q_{2}\in\dot{W}^{1,r}(\Gamma)
such that

\displaystyle-{\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}}(2\nu_{\Gamma}\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w})=\mathbf{A}^{\Gamma}(\mathbf{w})-\nabla_{\Gamma}q_{2}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{a.e.\penalty\ on $\Gamma$.}

(5.27)

Defining
q\coloneqq q_{1}+q_{2}
and combining (
5.24
), (
5.26
) and (
5.27
), we conclude that

\displaystyle-{\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}}(2\nu_{\Gamma}\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w})+\gamma\mathbf{w}+\nabla_{\Gamma}q=\mathbf{A}^{\Gamma}(\mathbf{w})+\gamma\mathbf{w}+\nabla_{\Gamma}q_{1}=\mathbf{f}_{\Gamma}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{a.e.\penalty\ on $\Gamma$.}

(5.28)

Moreover, the choice of
q
is unique if we additionally demand
{\langle q\rangle}_{\Gamma}=0
. Then, the Poincaré–Wirtinger inequality yields
q\in W^{1,r}(\Gamma)
. In this way,
(\mathbf{w},q)
is the unique strong solution of (
5.21
) with the desired regularities.
∎

In order to analyze the bulk-surface Stokes system (
5.1
), we also need the Helmholtz projection in the bulk. It is defined as

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{\textnormal{div}}^{\Omega}:\mathbf{L}^{r}(\Omega)\rightarrow\mathbf{L}^{r}_{\textnormal{div}}(\Omega),\hskip 10.00002pt\mathbf{P}_{\textnormal{div}}^{\Omega}(\mathbf{v})=\mathbf{v}-\nabla p,

(5.29)

where
p\in\dot{W}^{1,r}(\Omega)
is (up to an additive constant) uniquely determined by

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\nabla p\cdot\nabla\zeta\;\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\Omega}\mathbf{v}\cdot\nabla\zeta\;\mathrm{d}x\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for all $\zeta\in\dot{W}^{1,r^{\prime}}(\Omega)$},

cf.
[
72
, Theorem 1.4]
. Here,
r^{\prime}
denotes again the dual Sobolev exponent of
r
.

We are now in a position to prove the existence of a strong solution to (
5.1
).

Theorem 5.7
.

Let
\mathbf{f}\in\mathbf{L}^{r}(\Omega)
and
\mathbf{f}_{\Gamma}\in\mathbf{L}^{r}_{\tau}(\Gamma)
. Then there exists a unique solution
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\in\mathbfcal{W}^{2,r}_{0,\textnormal{div}}
and
(p,q)\in\mathcal{W}^{1,r}\cap\mathcal{L}^{r}_{(0)}
to the bulk-surface Stokes system (
5.1
). Moreover, there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\|(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\|_{\mathbfcal{W}^{2,r}}+\|(p,q)\|_{\mathcal{W}^{1,r}}\leq C\|(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{f}_{\Gamma})\|_{\mathbfcal{L}^{r}}.

(5.30)

Let
\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}\in\mathbf{W}^{2-1/r,r}_{\textnormal{div}}(\Gamma)
be arbitrary.
Then, according to
[
76
, Proposition 2.2 and Proposition 2.3]
, there exists a unique strong solution
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbf{W}^{2,r}_{\textnormal{div}}(\Omega)
and
p\in W^{1,r}(\Omega)\cap L^{r}_{(0)}(\Omega)
of the bulk Stokes problem

\displaystyle-\textnormal{div}(2\nu_{\Omega}\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v})+\nabla p

\displaystyle=\mathbf{f}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in\penalty\ }\Omega,

(5.31a)

\displaystyle\textnormal{div}\,\mathbf{v}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in\penalty\ }\Omega,

(5.31b)

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}|_{\Gamma}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Gamma.

(5.31c)

Furthermore,
[
76
, Proposition 2.2]
provides the estimate

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{v}\|_{\mathbf{W}^{2,r}(\Omega)}+\|p\|_{W^{1,r}(\Omega)}\leq C\big(\|\mathbf{f}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{r}(\Omega)}+\|\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}\|_{\mathbf{W}^{2-1/r,r}(\Gamma)}\big).

(5.32)

By the trace theorem, we have
\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}\in L^{r}(\Gamma;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
and thus,

\displaystyle\mathbf{f}_{\Gamma}-2\nu_{\Omega}\big[\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}\,\mathbf{n}\big]_{\tau}\in\mathbf{L}^{r}_{\tau}(\Gamma).

Hence, according to Theorem
5.6
, there exists a unique strong solution
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbf{W}^{2,r}_{\textnormal{div}}(\Gamma)
and
q\in W^{1,r}(\Gamma)
of the surface Stokes problem

\displaystyle-{\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}}(2\nu_{\Gamma}\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w})+\nabla_{\Gamma}q+\gamma\mathbf{w}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{f}_{\Gamma}-2\nu_{\Omega}\big[\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}\,\mathbf{n}\big]_{\tau}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Gamma,

(5.33a)

\displaystyle\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}\,\mathbf{w}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on\penalty\ }\Gamma.

(5.33b)

This defines an operator
T:\mathbf{W}^{2-1/r,r}_{\textnormal{div}}(\Gamma)\rightarrow\mathbf{W}^{2,r}_{\textnormal{div}}(\Gamma)
,
\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}\mapsto T\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}=\mathbf{w}
.
Using the estimates (
5.23
) and (
5.32
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|T\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{1}-T\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{2}\|_{\mathbf{W}^{2,r}(\Gamma)}&\leq C\|\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}_{1}\,\mathbf{n}-\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}_{2}\,\mathbf{n}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{r}(\Gamma)}\leq C\|\mathbf{v}_{1}-\mathbf{v}_{2}\|_{\mathbf{W}^{2,r}(\Omega)}\\
&\leq C\|\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{1}-\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{2}\|_{\mathbf{W}^{2-1/r,r}(\Gamma)}.\end{split}

(5.34)

This means that
T
is continuous.
Furthermore, since
\mathbf{W}^{2,r}(\Gamma)
is compactly embedded in
\mathbf{W}^{2-1/r,r}(\Gamma)
, Schauder’s fixed-point theorem implies that
T
has a fixed-point
\mathbf{w}_{\ast}\in\mathbf{W}^{2,r}_{\textnormal{div}}(\Gamma)
such that
T\mathbf{w}_{\ast}=\mathbf{w}_{\ast}
.
Finally, we write
q_{*}
to denote the pressure associated with
\mathbf{w}_{\ast}
, and we define
(\mathbf{v}_{\ast},p_{\ast})
as the corresponding unique strong solution to the bulk Stokes problem (
5.31
). In this way, we have constructed functions

\displaystyle(\mathbf{v}_{\ast},\mathbf{w}_{\ast})\in\mathbfcal{W}^{2,r}_{0,\textnormal{div}},\hskip 10.00002pt(p_{\ast},q_{\ast})\in\mathcal{W}^{1,r}\cap\mathcal{L}^{r}_{(0)},

which are a strong solution to the bulk-surface Stokes system (
5.1
). As every strong solution is also a weak solution, we conclude from Theorem
5.4
that the strong solution is unique. As a last step, we have to verify the inequality (
5.30
).
To this end, we recall the trace embedding
W^{3/4,r}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L^{r}(\Gamma)
(see, e.g.,
[
17
, Part I, Chapter 2, Theorem 2.24]
), which holds since
\tfrac{3}{4}>\tfrac{1}{2}\geq\tfrac{1}{r}
.
Using this embedding along with Sobolev embeddings, Ehrling’s lemma and estimate (
5.12
), we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}_{\ast}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{r}(\Gamma)}&\leq C\,\|\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}_{\ast}\|_{\mathbf{W}^{3/4,r}(\Omega)}\leq C\,\|\mathbf{v}_{\ast}\|_{\mathbf{W}^{7/4,r}(\Omega)}\\
&\leq\varepsilon\|\mathbf{v}_{\ast}\|_{\mathbf{W}^{2,r}(\Omega)}+C_{\varepsilon}\|\mathbf{v}_{\ast}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Omega)}\\
&\leq\varepsilon\|\mathbf{v}_{\ast}\|_{\mathbf{W}^{2,r}(\Omega)}+C_{\varepsilon}\,C\,\|(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{f}_{\Gamma})\|_{\mathbfcal{L}^{r}}\end{split}

(5.35)

for any
\varepsilon>0
and some constant
C_{\varepsilon}>0
that may depend on
\varepsilon
and the same quantities as constants denoted by
C
. Fixing a sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
,
the desired inequality (
5.30
) follows by combining (
5.35
) with the estimates (
5.23
) and (
5.32
). This completes the proof.
∎

5.3  The bulk-surface Stokes operator: Regularity and spectral theory

We further consider the situation where
\nu_{\Omega},\nu_{\Gamma}
and
\gamma
are positive constants.
Using the bulk and surface Helmholtz projections, we define the bulk-surface Stokes operator as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbfcal{A}:D(\mathbfcal{A})\subset\mathbfcal{L}^{2}_{\textnormal{div}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathbfcal{L}^{2}_{\textnormal{div}},

\displaystyle(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})

\displaystyle\mapsto\big(-\mathbf{P}_{\textnormal{div}}^{\Omega}(\textnormal{div}(2\nu_{\Omega}\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v})),-\mathbf{P}_{\textnormal{div}}^{\Gamma}({\textnormal{div}_{\Gamma}}(2\nu_{\Gamma}\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w})+2\nu_{\Omega}\big[\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}\,\mathbf{n}\big]_{\tau}+\gamma\mathbf{w})\big),

where
D(\mathbfcal{A})=\mathbfcal{H}^{2}_{0,\textnormal{div}}
.

Lemma 5.8
.

The operator
\mathbfcal{A}
is invertible and its inverse

\displaystyle\mathbfcal{S}:\mathbfcal{L}^{2}_{\textnormal{div}}\to\mathbfcal{L}^{2}_{\textnormal{div}},\hskip 10.00002pt\mathbfcal{S}(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{f}_{\Gamma})=\mathbfcal{A}^{-1}(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{f}_{\Gamma}),

which maps
(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{f}_{\Gamma})
onto the corresponding strong solution of the bulk-surface Stokes problem (
5.1
), is a linear and bounded operator that is injective, compact, and self-adjoint with respect to the inner product of
\mathbfcal{L}^{2}
.

The invertibility of
\mathbfcal{A}
and the existence of the inverse Stokes operator
\mathbfcal{S}
are a direct consequence of Theorem
5.7
. As the Stokes operator
\mathbfcal{A}
is linear and invertible, it is clear that
\mathbfcal{S}
is linear and injective.
Combining the estimate (
5.30
) with the trace embedding
\mathbf{H}^{2}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma)
, we deduce that
\mathbfcal{S}
is bounded. Moreover, as
\mathbfcal{S}(\mathbfcal{L}^{2}_{\textnormal{div}})=D(\mathbfcal{A})=\mathbfcal{H}^{2}_{0,\textnormal{div}}
, we conclude from the compact embedding
\mathbfcal{H}^{2}\hookrightarrow\mathbfcal{L}^{2}
that
\mathbfcal{S}
is compact. It remains to prove that
\mathbfcal{S}
is self-adjoint. To this end, let
(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{f}_{\Gamma}),(\widehat{\mathbf{f}},\widehat{\mathbf{f}}_{\Gamma})\in\mathbfcal{L}^{2}_{\textnormal{div}}
be arbitrary. Choosing
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})=\mathbfcal{S}(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{f}_{\Gamma})\in\mathbfcal{H}^{2}_{0,\textnormal{div}}
and
(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}})=\mathbfcal{S}(\widehat{\mathbf{f}},\widehat{\mathbf{f}}_{\Gamma})\in\mathbfcal{H}^{2}_{0,\textnormal{div}}
in the weak formulation (
5.11
), we finally conclude that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\big(\mathbfcal{S}(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{f}_{\Gamma}),(\widehat{\mathbf{f}},\widehat{\mathbf{f}}_{\Gamma})\big)_{\mathbfcal{L}^{2}}=\big<(\widehat{\mathbf{f}},\widehat{\mathbf{f}}_{\Gamma}),(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\big>_{\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0}}\\
&\quad=\int_{\Omega}2\nu_{\Omega}\,\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}:\mathbf{D}\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}2\nu_{\Gamma}\,\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}:\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma+\int_{\Gamma}\gamma\,\mathbf{w}\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma\\
&\quad=\big<(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{f}_{\Gamma}),(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}})\big>_{\mathbfcal{H}^{1}_{0}}=\big((\mathbf{f},\mathbf{f}_{\Gamma}),\mathbfcal{S}(\widehat{\mathbf{f}},\widehat{\mathbf{f}}_{\Gamma})\big)_{\mathbfcal{L}^{2}}.\end{split}

(5.36)

This proves that
\mathbfcal{S}
is self-adjoint with respect to the inner product of
\mathbfcal{L}^{2}
, and thus, the proof is complete.
∎

Theorem 5.9
.

The operator
\mathbfcal{A}
has countably many eigenvalues, and each of them has a finite-dimensional eigenspace. Repeating each eigenvalue according to its multiplicity, we can interpret them as a sequence
\{\lambda_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathbb{R}
with

\displaystyle 0<\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}\leq\lambda_{3}\leq\ldots\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003pt\lambda_{k}\to\infty\;\;\text{as $k\to\infty$}.

Moreover, there exists an orthonormal basis
\{(\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{k},\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathbfcal{H}^{2}_{0,\textnormal{div}}
of
\mathbfcal{L}^{2}_{\textnormal{div}}
such that for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
(\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{k},\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{k})
is an eigenfunction to the eigenvalue
\lambda_{k}
. This means that
(\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{k},\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{k})
is non-trivial and satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbfcal{A}(\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{k},\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{k})=\lambda_{k}(\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{k},\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{k})\hskip 10.00002pt\Leftrightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt(\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{k},\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{k})=\lambda_{k}\mathbfcal{S}(\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{k},\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{k}).

In particular, any pair
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\in\mathbfcal{L}^{2}_{\textnormal{div}}
can be expressed as

\displaystyle(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\big((\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w}),(\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{k},\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{k})\big)_{\mathbfcal{L}^{2}}(\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{k},\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{k})

in the sense that the series converges in
\mathbfcal{L}^{2}
.

Due to the properties established in Lemma
5.8
, the assertions directly follow by applying the spectral theorem for compact normal operators to the solution operator
\mathbfcal{S}
(see, e.g.,
[
10
, Sect. 2.12]
and also
[
25
, Secs. D.5–D.6]
).
∎

Corollary 5.10
.

The bulk-surface Helmholtz projection

\displaystyle\mathbfcal{P}_{\textnormal{div}}:\mathbfcal{L}^{2}\to\mathbfcal{L}^{2}_{\textnormal{div}},\hskip 10.00002pt\mathbfcal{P}_{\textnormal{div}}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})=\big(\mathbf{P}^{\Omega}_{\textnormal{div}}(\mathbf{v}),\mathbf{P}^{\Gamma}_{\textnormal{div}}(\mathbf{w})\big)

can be represented as

\displaystyle\mathbfcal{P}_{\textnormal{div}}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\big((\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w}),(\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{k},\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{k})\big)_{\mathbfcal{L}^{2}}(\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{k},\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{k}),

for all
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\in\mathbfcal{L}^{2}
, in the sense that the series converges in
\mathbfcal{L}^{2}
.

As
\mathbfcal{P}_{\textnormal{div}}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\in\mathbfcal{L}^{2}_{\textnormal{div}}
, the claim directly follows from the fact that, according to Theorem
5.9
, the eigenfunctions
\{(\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{k},\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
form an orthonormal basis of
\mathbfcal{L}^{2}_{\textnormal{div}}
.
∎

Corollary 5.11
.

The eigenfunctions
\{(\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{k},\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathbfcal{H}^{2}_{0,\textnormal{div}}
form a complete orthogonal system of
D(\mathbfcal{A})=\mathbfcal{H}^{2}_{0,\textnormal{div}}
.

First note that
\mathbfcal{A}
is self-adjoint, which can be shown by performing similar computations as for
\mathbfcal{S}
. The claim then follows as in the proof of
[
16
, Theorem II.6.6]
.
∎

Corollary 5.12
.

The inclusion
\mathbfcal{W}^{2,r}_{0,\textnormal{div}}\hookrightarrow\mathbfcal{L}^{2}_{\textnormal{div}}
is dense for every
r\in(2,\infty)
.

Let
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\in\mathbfcal{L}^{2}_{\textnormal{div}}
be arbitrary.
Since
\mathbfcal{H}^{2}_{0,\textnormal{div}}\hookrightarrow\mathbfcal{L}^{r}_{\textnormal{div}}
, Theorem
5.7
implies that

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{k},\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{k})=\lambda_{k}\mathbfcal{S}(\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{k},\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{k})\in\mathbfcal{W}^{2,r}_{0,\textnormal{div}}

for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Hence, for
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbfcal{W}^{2,r}_{0,\textnormal{div}}\ni\sum_{k=1}^{N}\big((\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w}),(\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{k},\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{k})\big)_{\mathbfcal{L}^{2}}(\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{k},\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{k})\to(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\hskip 10.00002pt\text{in $\mathbfcal{L}^{2}$}

as
N\to\infty
, which proves the claim.
∎

6  Construction of a weak solution for
L>0

In this section, we prove the following theorem, which ensures the existence of weak solutions to (
4.3
) in the sense of Definition
4.1
in the case
L\in(0,\infty]
.

Theorem 6.1
.

Suppose that the assumptions
(A1)
-
(A5)
hold. Let
K\in[0,\infty]
,
L\in(0,\infty]
,
let
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})\in\mathbfcal{L}^{2}_{\textnormal{div}}
, and let
(\phi_{0},\psi_{0})\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{K,\alpha}
satisfy (
4.4
).
Then there exists at least one weak solution
(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w},\phi,\psi,\mu,\theta)
to system (
4.3
) in the sense of Definition
4.1
.

The proof is based on a semi-Galerkin discretization of the Navier–Stokes equations combined with Schauder’s fixed-point theorem. To this end, we start by introducing an approximate system. In its construction, the analysis of the previous chapter on the bulk-surface Stokes equations will play a crucial role.

In this proof, the letter
C
will denote generic positive constants, which may depend only on
\Omega
,
T
, the initial data, and the constants introduced in
(A1)
-
(A5)
. The exact value of
C
may vary throughout the proof.

6.1  Construction of an approximate system

Let us consider the family of eigenfunctions
\{(\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{j},\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{j})\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
of the bulk-surface Stokes operator
\mathbfcal{A}
that was obtained in Theorem
5.9
. For any
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we introduce the finite-dimensional subspace
\mathbfcal{V}_{m}\coloneqq\textup{span}\{\left(\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{1}\right),\ldots,\left(\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{m},\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{m}\right)\}
of
\mathbfcal{L}^{2}_{\textnormal{div}}
. These finite-dimensional spaces
\mathbfcal{V}_{m}
are endowed with the norm of
\mathbfcal{L}^{2}
. The orthogonal projection of
\mathbfcal{L}^{2}
onto
\mathbfcal{V}_{m}
with respect to the inner product on
\mathbfcal{L}^{2}_{\textnormal{div}}
is denoted by
\mathbfcal{P}_{\mathbfcal{V}_{m}}=(\mathbf{P}_{\mathbfcal{V}_{m}}^{\Omega},\mathbf{P}_{\mathbfcal{V}_{m}}^{\Gamma})
. Recalling that
\Omega
is of class
C^{4}
, the regularity theory of the bulk-surface Stokes operator
\mathbfcal{A}
(see Theorem
5.7
) yields that
(\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{j},\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{j})\in\mathbfcal{W}^{2,r}_{0,\textnormal{div}}
for all
r\in[2,\infty)
and
j\in\mathbb{N}
. Moreover, invoking the estimate (
5.30
), it is straightforward to check that the following inverse Sobolev embedding inequalities hold for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
r\in[2,\infty)
and
(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}})\in\mathbfcal{V}_{m}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}})\|_{\mathbfcal{H}^{1}}&\leq C_{m}\|(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}})\|_{\mathbfcal{L}^{2}},\quad\|(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}})\|_{\mathbfcal{H}^{2}}\leq C_{m}\|(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}})\|_{\mathbfcal{L}^{2}},\\
\|(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}})\|_{\mathbfcal{W}^{2,r}}&\leq C_{m,r}\|(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}})\|_{\mathbfcal{L}^{2}}.\end{split}

(6.1)

Here,
C_{m}
and
C_{m,r}
are positive constants that may depend on the same quantities as
C
and additionally on
m
and
(m,r)
, respectively.
Now, we claim that, for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists an approximate solution
(\mathbf{v}_{m},\mathbf{w}_{m},\phi_{m},\psi_{m},\mu_{m},\theta_{m})
to system (
4.3
) having the regularities

\displaystyle\left\{\;\begin{aligned} (\mathbf{v}_{m},\mathbf{w}_{m})&\in H^{1}(0,T;\mathbfcal{V}_{m}),\\
(\phi_{m},\psi_{m})&\in H^{1}(0,T;(\mathcal{H}^{1})^{\prime})\cap L^{\infty}(0,T;\mathcal{H}^{1}_{K,\alpha})\cap L^{2}(0,T;\mathcal{W}^{2,6}),\\
(\mu_{m},\theta_{m})&\in L^{2}(0,T;\mathcal{H}^{1}),\\
(F^{\prime}(\phi_{m}),G^{\prime}(\psi_{m}))&\in L^{2}(0,T;\mathcal{L}^{6}),\end{aligned}\right.

(6.2)

with
\left|\phi_{m}\right|<1
a.e. in
Q
and
\left|\psi_{m}\right|<1
a.e. on
\Sigma
, in the sense that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\rho(\phi_{m})\partial_{t}\mathbf{v}_{m}\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}\sigma(\psi_{m})\partial_{t}\mathbf{w}_{m}\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\int_{\Omega}\rho(\phi_{m})(\mathbf{v}_{m}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{v}_{m}\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}\sigma(\psi_{m})(\mathbf{w}_{m}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma})\mathbf{w}_{m}\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\int_{\Omega}\rho^{\prime}\,(\nabla\mu_{m}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{v}_{m}\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}\sigma^{\prime}\,(\nabla_{\Gamma}\theta_{m}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma})\mathbf{w}_{m}\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma

(6.3)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\int_{\Omega}2\nu_{\Omega}(\phi_{m})\mathbf{D}\mathbf{v}_{m}:\mathbf{D}\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}2\nu_{\Gamma}(\psi_{m})\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{w}_{m}:\mathbf{D}_{\Gamma}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma+\int_{\Gamma}\gamma(\phi_{m},\psi_{m})\mathbf{w}_{m}\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt=\int_{\Omega}\mu_{m}\nabla\phi_{m}\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{v}}\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Gamma}\theta_{m}\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi_{m}\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\frac{\chi(L)}{2}\int_{\Gamma}(\beta\sigma^{\prime}-\rho^{\prime})(\beta\theta_{m}-\mu_{m})\mathbf{w}_{m}\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma

a.e. on
[0,T]
for all
(\widehat{\mathbf{v}},\widehat{\mathbf{w}})\in\mathbfcal{V}_{m}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\big<(\partial_{t}\phi_{m},\partial_{t}\psi_{m}),(\zeta,\xi)\big>_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}-\int_{\Omega}\phi_{m}\mathbf{v}_{m}\cdot\nabla\zeta\;\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\Gamma}\psi_{m}\mathbf{w}_{m}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\xi\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma\\
&\quad=-\int_{\Omega}\nabla\mu_{m}\cdot\nabla\zeta\;\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\Gamma}\nabla_{\Gamma}\theta_{m}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\xi\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma-\chi(L)\int_{\Gamma}(\beta\theta_{m}-\mu_{m})(\beta\xi-\zeta)\;\mathrm{d}\Gamma\end{split}

(6.4)

a.e. on
[0,T]
for all
(\zeta,\xi)\in\mathcal{H}^{1}
,

\displaystyle\mu_{m}=-\Delta\phi_{m}+F^{\prime}(\phi_{m})

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\text{a.e.\penalty\ in\penalty\ }Q,

(6.5a)
